| Dates | Described as a pandemic by the [WHO](33583.md): 11 March 2020 (6 years and 6 months ago)[^cite_note-start-3]  [Public health emergency of international concern](42694003.md): 30 January 2020 – 5 May 2023 (3 years, 3 months and 5 days)[^cite_note-reuters-4] |
| Confirmed cases | 779,277,425[^cite_note-Template\:COVID-19_data-5][^cite_note-6] |

| Deaths | 7,115,706[^cite_note-Template\:COVID-19_data-5] (reported) 18.5–35.2 million[^cite_note-Economist2024-9] (estimated) |
| Fatality rate | As of 10 March 2023: 1.02%[^cite_note-JHU_ticker-10] |

The global **COVID-19 pandemic** (also known as the **coronavirus pandemic**), caused by severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 ([SARS-CoV-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2)), began with an outbreak in [Wuhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan), China, in December 2019. It spread to other parts of Asia and [then worldwide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_by_country_and_territory) in early 2020. The [World Health Organization](33583.md) (WHO) declared the outbreak a [public health emergency of international concern](42694003.md) (PHEIC) on 30 January 2020 and assessed it as having become a [pandemic](24255.md) on 11 March.[^cite_note-start-3] The WHO declared that the public health emergency caused by COVID-19 had ended in May 2023, while noting that COVID-19 continued to be a global health threat.[^cite_note-reuters-4]

[COVID-19 symptoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_symptoms) range from [asymptomatic](602790.md) to deadly, but most commonly include fever, [sore throat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sore_throat), [nocturnal cough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nocturnal_cough), and fatigue. [Transmission of the virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transmission_of_COVID-19) is often [through airborne particles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Airborne_transmission). Mutations have [produced many strains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variants_of_SARS-CoV-2) (variants) with varying degrees of infectivity and [virulence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virulence).[^cite_note-11] [COVID-19 vaccines](63319438.md) were developed rapidly and [deployed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines) to the general public beginning in December 2020, made available through government and international programmes such as [COVAX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVAX), aiming to provide [vaccine equity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_equity). [Treatments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treatment_and_management_of_COVID-19) include [novel antiviral drugs](63435931.md) and symptom control. Common mitigation measures during the public health emergency included [travel restrictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_restrictions_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic), [lockdowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdowns), business restrictions and closures, [workplace hazard controls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Workplace_hazard_controls_for_COVID-19), [mask mandates](63631542.md), quarantines, [testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_testing) systems, and [contact tracing](1724851.md) of the infected.

The pandemic caused severe [social](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic) and [economic disruption](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic) around the world, including [the largest global recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_recession) since the [Great Depression](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Depression).[^cite_note-RFXoH-12] [Widespread supply shortages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic), including [food shortages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_security_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic), were caused by [supply chain disruptions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021%E2%80%932022_global_supply_chain_crisis) and [panic buying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panic_buying). Reduced human activity led to an [unprecedented temporary decrease in pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_the_environment). Educational institutions and public areas were partially or fully [closed in many jurisdictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_education), and many events were cancelled or postponed during 2020 and 2021. [Telework](158555.md) became much more common for [white-collar workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White-collar_worker) as the pandemic evolved. [Misinformation circulated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_misinformation) through social media and occasionally through mass media, and [political tensions intensified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic). The pandemic raised issues of [racial and geographic discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophobia_and_racism_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic), [health equity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_equity), and the balance between [public health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health) imperatives and [individual rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Individual_and_group_rights).

The disease has continued to circulate since 2023. By 2025, experts generally believed the pandemic to be over, having transitioned into the [endemic phase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic_\(epidemiology\)).[^cite_note-\:0-13][^cite_note-\:3-14] Different definitions of pandemics lead to different determinations of when they end.[^cite_note-timemarch2024-15][^cite_note-charters2021-16] As of 18 September 2026, COVID-19 has caused 7,115,706[^cite_note-Template\:COVID-19_data-5] confirmed deaths and 18.5 to 35.2 million estimated deaths.[^cite_note-Economist2024-9]

## Terminology

Further information: [COVID-19 naming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_naming)



Chinese medics in [Huanggang](4088485.md), Hubei, in 2020

### Pandemic

In [epidemiology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epidemiology), a [pandemic](24255.md) is defined as "an epidemic occurring over a very wide area, crossing international boundaries, and usually affecting a large number of people". During the COVID-19 pandemic, as with other pandemics, the meaning of this term has been challenged.[^cite_note-17]

The end of a pandemic or other epidemic only rarely involves the total disappearance of a disease and, historically, much less attention has been given to defining the ends of epidemics than their beginnings. The ends of particular epidemics have been defined in a variety of ways, differing according to academic field, and differently based on location and social group. An epidemic's end can be considered a social phenomenon, not just a biological one.[^cite_note-charters2021-16]

[*Time*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\)) reported in March 2024 that expert opinions differ on whether COVID-19 is currently considered endemic or pandemic, and that the WHO continued to call the disease a pandemic on its website.[^cite_note-timemarch2024-15]

### Virus names

During [the initial outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Hubei) in [Wuhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan), the virus and disease were commonly referred to as "coronavirus", "Wuhan coronavirus",[^cite_note-18] "the coronavirus outbreak" and the "Wuhan coronavirus outbreak",[^cite_note-19] with the disease sometimes called "Wuhan [pneumonia](52135.md)".[^cite_note-20][^cite_note-21] In January 2020, the WHO recommended 2019-nCoV[^cite_note-WHO_report-22] and 2019-nCoV acute respiratory disease[^cite_note-23] as interim names for the virus and disease per 2015 international guidelines against using geographical locations (e.g. Wuhan, China), animal species, or groups of people in disease and virus names in part to prevent [social stigma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_stigma).[^cite_note-24] The WHO finalised the official names COVID-19 and SARS-CoV-2 on 11 February 2020.[^cite_note-WHO-naming-25] WHO Director-General [Tedros Ghebreyesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tedros_Ghebreyesus) explained: CO for *corona*, VI for *virus*, D for *disease* and 19 for when the outbreak was first identified (31 December 2019).[^cite_note-26] The WHO additionally uses "the COVID-19 virus" and "the virus responsible for COVID-19" in public communications.[^cite_note-WHO-naming-25]

The WHO named [variants of concern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variant_of_concern) and variants of interest using [Greek letters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_alphabet). The initial practice of naming them according to where the variants were identified (e.g. [Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Delta_variant) began as the "Indian variant") is no longer common.[^cite_note-27] A more systematic naming scheme reflects the variant's [PANGO lineage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phylogenetic_Assignment_of_Named_Global_Outbreak_Lineages) (e.g., [Omicron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant)'s lineage is B.1.1.529) and is used for other variants.[^cite_note-BBC.May.31.2021-28][^cite_note-29]

### Background

Main articles: [Origin of SARS-CoV-2](66389098.md) and [COVID-19 pandemic in Hubei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Hubei)

SARS-CoV-2 is a virus closely related to [bat coronaviruses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bat_virome#Coronaviruses),[^cite_note-LancetNowcasting-30] [pangolin](51124.md) coronaviruses,[^cite_note-ia56U-31][^cite_note-Zhang6April2020-32] and [SARS](196741.md).[^cite_note-ECDC_risk_assessment-33] The first known [outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outbreak) (the [2019–2020 COVID-19 outbreak in mainland China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%932020_COVID-19_outbreak_in_mainland_China)) started in [Wuhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan), Hubei, China, in December 2019.[^cite_note-34] Many early cases were linked to people who had visited the [Huanan Seafood Wholesale Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huanan_Seafood_Wholesale_Market) there,[^cite_note-Sun2020epidemiology-35][^cite_note-36][^cite_note-Maxmen2021whoReport-37] but it is possible that human-to-human transmission began earlier.[^cite_note-Hu2020natureReviews-38][^cite_note-Graham2020immunity-39] [Molecular clock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_clock) analysis suggests that the first cases were likely to have been between October and November 2019.[^cite_note-To2021lessons-40]

The scientific consensus is that the virus is most likely of a [zoonotic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zoonotic) origin, from bats or another closely related mammal.[^cite_note-Hu2020natureReviews-38][^cite_note-41][^cite_note-To2021lessons-40] While other explanations, such as speculations that SARS-CoV-2 was accidentally [released from a laboratory](66692273.md), have been proposed,[^cite_note-CovidMayHave-42][^cite_note-nofact-43][^cite_note-20200208economist-44] as of 2021 these were not sufficiently supported by evidence.[^cite_note-Holmes2021-45]

### Cases

Main articles: [COVID-19 pandemic by country and territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_by_country_and_territory) and [COVID-19 pandemic cases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_cases)

Official "case" counts refer to the number of people who have been [tested for COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_testing) and whose test has been confirmed positive according to official protocols whether they experienced symptomatic disease.[^cite_note-1ceLG-46][^cite_note-J0rix-47] Due to the effect of [sampling bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sampling_bias), studies which obtain a more accurate number by extrapolating from a random sample have consistently found that total infections considerably exceed the reported case counts.[^cite_note-WqXOo-48][^cite_note-BtFLV-49] Many countries, early on, had official policies to not test those with only mild symptoms.[^cite_note-elpais640000-50][^cite_note-20200313reuters-51] The strongest risk factors for severe illness are obesity, [complications of diabetes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Complications_of_diabetes), anxiety disorders, and the total number of co-morbid conditions.[^cite_note-52]

During the start of the COVID-19 pandemic, it was not clear whether young people were less likely to be infected, or less likely to develop symptoms and be tested.[^cite_note-vox21190033-53] A retrospective [cohort study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cohort_study) in China found that [children](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_children) and adults were just as likely to be infected.[^cite_note-489Q3-54]



Semi-log plot of weekly new cases of COVID-19 in the world and the top six countries in 2022

Among more thorough studies, [preliminary results](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_Case-Cluster-Study) from 9 April 2020 found that in [Gangelt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gangelt), the centre of a major infection cluster in Germany, 15 per cent of a population sample tested positive for [antibodies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antibody).[^cite_note-otW1O-55] Screening for COVID-19 in pregnant women [in New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_New_York_City), and [blood donors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_donor) in the Netherlands, found rates of positive antibody tests that indicated more infections than reported.[^cite_note-rCdvL-56][^cite_note-20200416reuters-57] [Seroprevalence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seroprevalence)-based estimates are conservative as some studies show that persons with mild symptoms do not have detectable antibodies.[^cite_note-0LgFK-58]

Initial estimates of the [basic reproduction number](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_reproduction_number) (R<sub>0</sub>) for COVID-19 in January 2020 were between 1.4 and 2.5,[^cite_note-WFNfK-59] but a subsequent analysis claimed that it may be about 5.7 (with a 95 per cent [confidence interval](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confidence_interval) of 3.8 to 8.9).[^cite_note-mmCQc-60]

In December 2021, the number of cases continued to climb due to several factors, including new COVID-19 variants. As of that 28 December, 282,790,822 individuals worldwide had been confirmed as infected.[^cite_note-61] As of 14 April 2022, over 500 million cases were confirmed globally.[^cite_note-62] Most cases are unconfirmed, with the [Institute for Health Metrics and Evaluation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Health_Metrics_and_Evaluation) estimating the true number of cases as of early 2022 to be in the billions.[^cite_note-57percent1-63][^cite_note-57percent2-64]

#### Test positivity rate

One measure that public health officials and policymakers have used to monitor the pandemic and guide decision-making is the test positivity rate ("percent positive"). According to Johns Hopkins in 2020, one benchmark for a "too high" per cent positive is 5%, which was used by the WHO in the past.[^cite_note-65]

### Deaths

Main articles: [COVID-19 pandemic deaths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_deaths) and [COVID-19 pandemic death rates by country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_death_rates_by_country)

Further information: [List of deaths due to COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_deaths_due_to_COVID-19)



Gravediggers bury the body of a man suspected of having died of COVID-19 in the cemetery of Vila Alpina in eastern [São Paulo](390875.md), 3 April 2020.



The deceased in a refrigerated "mobile morgue" outside a hospital in [Hackensack, New Jersey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hackensack,_New_Jersey), US, in April 2020



Global excess and reported COVID-19 deaths and deaths per 100,000, according to the WHO study

[^cite_note-10.1038/s41586-022-05522-2-66]

As of 6 September 2026, an estimated 7,116,673 deaths had been attributed to COVID-19.[^cite_note-67] The first confirmed death was in Wuhan on 9 January 2020.[^cite_note-AutoDW-25-68] These numbers vary by region and over time, influenced by testing volume, healthcare system quality, treatment options, government response,[^cite_note-69] time since the initial outbreak, and population characteristics, such as age, sex, and overall health.[^cite_note-worldindata-70]

Multiple measures are used to quantify mortality.[^cite_note-wBm2a-71] Official death counts typically include people who died after testing positive. Such counts exclude deaths without a test.[^cite_note-20200331reuters-72] Conversely, deaths of people who died from underlying conditions following a positive test may be included.[^cite_note-stuff120443722-73] Countries such as Belgium include deaths from suspected cases, including those without a test, thereby increasing counts.[^cite_note-npr841005901-74]

Official death counts have been claimed to underreport the actual death toll, because [excess mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mortality_displacement) (the number of deaths in a period compared to a long-term average) data show an increase in deaths that is not explained by COVID-19 deaths alone.[^cite_note-\:7-75] Using such data, estimates of the true number of deaths from COVID-19 worldwide have included a range from 18.5 to 35.2 million (≈27.4 million) by 25 January 2024 by *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)*,[^cite_note-Economist2024-9][^cite_note-\:7-75] as well as over 18.5 million by 1 April 2023 by the [Institute for Health Metrics and Evaluation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Health_Metrics_and_Evaluation)[^cite_note-COVID-19_Projections-76] and ≈18.2 million (earlier) deaths between 1 January 2020, and 31 December 2021, by a comprehensive international study.[^cite_note-10.1016/S0140-6736(21)02796-3-77] Such deaths include deaths due to healthcare capacity constraints and priorities, as well as reluctance to seek care (to avoid possible infection).[^cite_note-20200429reason-78] Further research may help distinguish the proportions directly caused by COVID-19 from those caused by indirect consequences of the pandemic.[^cite_note-10.1016/S0140-6736(21)02796-3-77]

In May 2022, the WHO estimated the number of excess deaths by the end of 2021 to be 14.9 million compared to 5.4 million reported COVID-19 deaths, with the majority of the unreported 9.5 million deaths believed to be direct deaths due to the virus, rather than indirect deaths. Some deaths were because [people with other conditions could not access medical services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_other_health_issues).[^cite_note-79][^cite_note-80]

A December 2022 [WHO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WHO) study estimated excess deaths from the pandemic during 2020 and 2021, again concluding ≈14.8 million excess early deaths occurred, reaffirming and detailing their prior calculations from May as well as updating them, addressing criticisms. These numbers do not include measures like [years of potential life lost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Years_of_potential_life_lost) and may make the pandemic 2021's [leading cause of death](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_causes_of_death_by_rate).[^cite_note-81][^cite_note-82][^cite_note-10.1038/s41586-022-05522-2-66]

The time between symptom onset and death ranges from 6 to 41 days, typically about 14 days.[^cite_note-pathogenesis-83] Mortality rates increase as a function of age. People at the greatest mortality risk are the elderly and those with underlying conditions.[^cite_note-84][^cite_note-6XONR-85]



Semi-log plot of weekly deaths due to COVID-19 in the world and top six current countries (mean with cases)

#### Infection fatality ratio (IFR)

See also: [List of human disease case fatality rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_human_disease_case_fatality_rates)

| Age group | IFR |
| --- | --- |
| 0–34 | 0.004% |
| 35–44 | 0.068% |
| 45–54 | 0.23% |
| 55–64 | 0.75% |
| 65–74 | 2.5% |
| 75–84 | 8.5% |
| 85 + | 28.3% |

The [infection fatality ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infection_fatality_rate) (IFR) is the cumulative number of deaths attributed to the disease divided by the cumulative number of infected individuals (including asymptomatic and undiagnosed infections and excluding vaccinated infected individuals). It is expressed in percentage points.[^cite_note-87] Other studies refer to this metric as the *infection fatality risk*.[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89]

In November 2020, a review article in *[Nature](43427.md)* reported estimates of population-weighted IFRs for various countries, excluding deaths in elderly care facilities, and found a median range of 0.24% to 1.49%.[^cite_note-90] IFRs rise as a function of age (from 0.002% at age 10 and 0.01% at age 25, to 0.4% at age 55, 1.4% at age 65, 4.6% at age 75, and 15% at age 85). These rates vary by a factor of ≈10,000 across the age groups.[^cite_note-EJE_levinetal-86] For comparison, the IFR for middle-aged adults is two orders of magnitude higher than the annualised risk of a fatal automobile accident and much higher than the risk of dying from [seasonal influenza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seasonal_influenza).[^cite_note-EJE_levinetal-86]

In December 2020, a systematic review and meta-analysis estimated that population-weighted IFR was 0.5% to 1% in some countries (France, Netherlands, New Zealand, and Portugal), 1% to 2% in other countries (Australia, England, Lithuania, and Spain), and about 2.5% in Italy. This study reported that most of the differences reflected corresponding differences in the population's age structure and the age-specific pattern of infections.[^cite_note-EJE_levinetal-86] There have also been reviews that have compared the fatality rate of this pandemic with prior pandemics, such as MERS-CoV.[^cite_note-91]

For comparison the infection mortality rate of seasonal flu in the United States is 0.1%, which is 13 times lower than COVID-19.[^cite_note-92]

#### Case fatality ratio (CFR)

Another metric in assessing death rate is the [case fatality ratio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Case_fatality_rate) (CFR),[^cite_note-94] which is the ratio of deaths to diagnoses. This metric can be misleading because of the delay between symptom onset and death and because testing focuses on symptomatic individuals.[^cite_note-Hauser_2020-95]

Based on [Johns Hopkins University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_University) statistics, the global CFR was 1.02 percent (6,881,955 deaths for 676,609,955 cases) as of 10 March 2023.[^cite_note-JHU_ticker-10] The number varies by region and has generally declined over time.[^cite_note-b0L7I-96]

## Disease

Main article: [COVID-19](63030231.md)

### Variants

Main article: [Variants of SARS-CoV-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variants_of_SARS-CoV-2)

Several [variants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variants_of_SARS-CoV-2) have been named by WHO and labelled as a [variant of concern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variant_of_concern) (VoC) or a [variant of interest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variant_of_interest) (VoI). Many of these variants have shared the more infectious [D614G](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/D614G). As of May 2023, the WHO had downgraded all variants of concern to previously circulating as these were no longer detected in new infections.[^cite_note-97][^cite_note-CDC2-98] Sub-lineages of the Omicron variant (BA.1 – BA.5) were considered separate VoCs by the WHO until they were downgraded in March 2023 as no longer widely circulating.[^cite_note-CDC2-98] As of 25 September 2026, only JN.1 is specified as a variant of interest by the WHO, while the following variants are under monitoring: PQ.16.1.1, NB.1.8.1, XFG, and BA.3.2.[^cite_note-99]

[World Health Organization](33583.md) video describing how variants proliferate in unvaccinated areas

| Name | Lineage | Detected | Countries | Priority |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [Alpha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Alpha_variant) | [B.1.1.7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lineage_B.1.1.7) | United Kingdom | 190 | VoC |
| [Beta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Beta_variant) | [B.1.351](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lineage_B.1.351) | South Africa | 140 | VoC |
| [Delta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Delta_variant) | [B.1.617.2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lineage_B.1.617.2) | India | 170 | VoC |
| [Gamma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Gamma_variant) | [P.1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lineage_P.1) | Brazil | 90 | VoC |
| [Omicron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant) | [B.1.1.529](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lineage_B.1.1.529) | Botswana | 149 | VoC |

### Signs and symptoms

Main article: [Symptoms of COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symptoms_of_COVID-19)



[Symptoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Signs_and_symptoms) of COVID-19

Symptoms of COVID-19 are variable, ranging from mild symptoms to severe illness.[^cite_note-CDC2020Sym-102][^cite_note-Grant_et_al_2020-103] Common symptoms include headache, [loss of smell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anosmia) and [taste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ageusia), [nasal congestion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasal_obstruction) and [runny nose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rhinorrhea), cough, [muscle pain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscle_pain), [sore throat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sore_throat), fever, [diarrhoea](53951.md), and [breathing difficulties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breathing_difficulties).[^cite_note-CDC2020Sym-102] People with the same infection may have different symptoms, and their symptoms may change over time. Three common clusters of symptoms have been identified: one respiratory symptom cluster with cough, [sputum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputum), shortness of breath, and fever; a musculoskeletal symptom cluster with muscle and joint pain, headache, and fatigue; a cluster of digestive symptoms with abdominal pain, vomiting, and diarrhoea.[^cite_note-ECDC-104] In people without prior ear, nose, and throat disorders, [loss of taste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hypogeusia) combined with [loss of smell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyposmia) is associated with [COVID-19](63030231.md) and is reported in as many as 88% of cases.[^cite_note-Krishnan-105][^cite_note-106][^cite_note-pmid32483687-107]

### Transmission

Main article: [Transmission of COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transmission_of_COVID-19)

The disease is mainly transmitted via the respiratory route when people inhale droplets and small airborne particles (that form an [aerosol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aerosol)) that infected people exhale as they breathe, talk, cough, sneeze, or sing.[^cite_note-Wang_2021-108][^cite_note-auto-109][^cite_note-AR2021-110][^cite_note-auto1-111] Infected people are more likely to transmit COVID-19 when they are physically close to other non-infected individuals. However, infection can occur over longer distances, particularly indoors.[^cite_note-Wang_2021-108][^cite_note-Miller_2021-112]

### Cause

Main article: [SARS-CoV-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2)



Illustration of SARS-CoV-2 [virion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virion)

SARS‑CoV‑2 (severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2) belongs to the broad family of viruses known as [coronaviruses](201983.md).[^cite_note-Fox2020-113] It is a [positive-sense single-stranded RNA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Positive-sense_single-stranded_RNA_virus) (+ssRNA) virus, with a single linear RNA segment. Coronaviruses infect humans, other mammals, including livestock and companion animals, and avian species.[^cite_note-V'kovskik\:21-114]

Human coronaviruses are capable of causing illnesses ranging from the [common cold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_cold) to more severe diseases such as [Middle East respiratory syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_respiratory_syndrome) (MERS, fatality rate ≈34%). SARS-CoV-2 is the seventh known coronavirus to infect people, after [229E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_coronavirus_229E), [NL63](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_coronavirus_NL63), [OC43](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_coronavirus_OC43), [HKU1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_coronavirus_HKU1), [MERS-CoV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_respiratory_syndrome-related_coronavirus), and the original [SARS-CoV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severe_acute_respiratory_syndrome_coronavirus).[^cite_note-NEJM-Novel-115]

### Diagnosis

Main article: [COVID-19 § Diagnosis](63030231.md#Diagnosis)



A nurse at [McMurdo Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/McMurdo_Station) sets up polymerase chain reaction (PCR) testing equipment in September 2020.

The standard method of testing for presence of SARS-CoV-2 is a [nucleic acid test](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nucleic_acid_test),[^cite_note-20200130cdc-116] which detects the presence of viral RNA fragments.[^cite_note-WHO_InterimGuidance-117] As these tests detect RNA but not infectious virus, its "ability to determine duration of infectivity of patients is limited".[^cite_note-2k0iS-118] The test is typically done on respiratory samples obtained by a [nasopharyngeal swab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nasopharyngeal_swab); however, a nasal swab or sputum sample may also be used.[^cite_note-CDC2020Testing-119][^cite_note-20200129cdc-120] The WHO has published several testing protocols for the disease.[^cite_note-1ceLG-46]

### Prevention

Further information: [COVID-19 § Prevention](63030231.md#Prevention), [Face masks during the COVID-19 pandemic](63631542.md), and [Social distancing measures related to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_distancing_measures_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)



Common measures implemented to prevent the spread of the virus

Preventive measures to reduce the chances of infection include getting vaccinated, staying at home, or spending more time outdoors, avoiding crowded places, keeping distance from others, wearing a mask in public, ventilating indoor spaces, managing potential exposure duration, washing hands with soap and water often and for at least twenty seconds, practising good respiratory hygiene, and avoiding touching the eyes, nose, or mouth with unwashed hands.[^cite_note-cdc-prev-121][^cite_note-who2023-122]

Those diagnosed with COVID-19 or who believe they may be infected are advised by healthcare authorities to stay home except to get medical care; call ahead before visiting a healthcare provider; wear a face mask before entering the healthcare provider's office; when in any room or vehicle with another person, cover coughs and sneezes with a tissue; regularly wash hands with soap and water; and avoid sharing personal household items.[^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124][^cite_note-125]

#### Vaccines

Main article: [COVID-19 vaccine](63319438.md)

See also: [History of COVID-19 vaccine development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_COVID-19_vaccine_development) and [Deployment of COVID-19 vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines)



An elderly woman receiving a COVID-19 vaccination in [Slovakia](26830.md)

A COVID-19 [vaccine](32653.md) is intended to provide [acquired immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acquired_immunity) against [SARS-CoV-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2), the virus that causes [COVID-19](63030231.md). Prior to the COVID-19 pandemic, an established body of knowledge existed about the structure and function of [coronaviruses](201983.md) causing diseases like [severe acute respiratory syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Severe_acute_respiratory_syndrome) (SARS) and [Middle East respiratory syndrome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_respiratory_syndrome) (MERS). This knowledge accelerated the development of various [vaccine platforms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_platform) during early 2020.[^cite_note-pmid33341119-126]

The initial focus of SARS-CoV-2 vaccines was on preventing symptomatic and severe illness.[^cite_note-127] The COVID-19 vaccines are widely credited for their role in reducing the severity and death caused by COVID-19.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129]

As of August 2024, more than 5.6 billion people had received one or more doses (13.7 billion in total).[^cite_note-130] The Oxford-AstraZeneca vaccine was the most widely used.[^cite_note-131] According to a June 2022 study, COVID-19 vaccines prevented an additional 14.4 million to 19.8 million deaths in 185 countries and territories from 8 December 2020 to 8 December 2021.[^cite_note-132][^cite_note-133]

In 2022, the first recombinant protein-based COVID-19 vaccine (the [Novavax vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novavax_COVID-19_vaccine)) was authorised for use in adults in the United Kingdom. It has subsequently received endorsement/authorisation from the WHO, US, European Union, and Australia.[^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135] That same year also saw the first inhalable vaccine, developed by Chinese biopharmaceutical company [CanSino Biologics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CanSino_Biologics#COVID-19_vaccine_development).[^cite_note-136]

[High-income countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-income_economy) rapidly bought up vaccines at the start of the pandemic, leading to concerns about [vaccine equity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vaccine_equity). By April 2021, one in 500 people in [low and middle-income countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low_and_middle-income_countries) had been vaccinated, compared with one in four in high-income countries.[^cite_note-137]

### Treatment

Main article: [Treatment and management of COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Treatment_and_management_of_COVID-19)



A critically ill patient receiving invasive ventilation in the intensive care unit of the [Heart Institute, University of São Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heart_Institute,_University_of_S%C3%A3o_Paulo) in July 2020. Due to a shortage of mechanical ventilators, a [bridge ventilator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Resuscitator) is being used to automatically actuate a [bag valve mask](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bag_valve_mask).

For the first two years of the pandemic, no specific and effective treatment or cure was available.[^cite_note-BMJLivingReview-138][^cite_note-cdcmuseum-139] In 2021, the [European Medicines Agency's (EMA)](1427054.md) Committee for Medicinal Products for Human Use (CHMP) approved the oral antiviral [protease inhibitor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protease_inhibitor_\(pharmacology\)), [Paxlovid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paxlovid) (nirmatrelvir plus the HIV antiviral [ritonavir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ritonavir)), to treat adult patients.[^cite_note-140] The FDA later gave it an [emergency use authorization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emergency_Use_Authorization) (EUA).[^cite_note-141]

Most cases of COVID-19 are mild. In these, supportive care includes medication such as [paracetamol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paracetamol) or [non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonsteroidal_anti-inflammatory_drug) to relieve symptoms (fever,[^cite_note-142] body aches, cough), adequate intake of oral fluids, and rest.[^cite_note-cdcmuseum-139][^cite_note-143] Good personal hygiene and a [healthy diet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Healthy_diet) are also recommended.[^cite_note-144]

[Supportive care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supportive_care) in severe cases includes treatment to [relieve symptoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symptomatic_treatment), [fluid therapy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fluid_replacement), [oxygen support](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxygen_support), [prone positioning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prone_positioning), and medications or devices to support other affected vital organs.[^cite_note-145] More severe cases may need treatment in hospital. In those with low oxygen levels, use of the glucocorticoid [dexamethasone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dexamethasone) is recommended to reduce mortality.[^cite_note-146] [Noninvasive ventilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noninvasive_ventilation) and, ultimately, admission to an [intensive care unit](6332859.md) for [mechanical ventilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanical_ventilation) may be required to support breathing.[^cite_note-NIHGuidelines2020-147] [Extracorporeal membrane oxygenation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extracorporeal_membrane_oxygenation) (ECMO) has been used to address the issue of respiratory failure.[^cite_note-Guan_Ni_Hu_Liang_p.-148][^cite_note-Henry_2020_p.-149]

Existing drugs such as [hydroxychloroquine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroxychloroquine), [lopinavir/ritonavir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lopinavir/ritonavir), and [ivermectin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivermectin) are not recommended by US or European health authorities, as there is no good evidence they have any useful effect.[^cite_note-BMJLivingReview-138][^cite_note-Kim_Read_Fauci_p=2149-150][^cite_note-NIHGuidelinesTherapeuticManagement-151] The antiviral [remdesivir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Remdesivir) is available in the US, Canada, Australia, and several other countries, with varying restrictions; however, it is not recommended for use with mechanical ventilation, and is discouraged altogether by the WHO[^cite_note-Hsu_p=m4457-152] due to limited evidence of its efficacy.[^cite_note-BMJLivingReview-138]

### Prognosis

Further information: [COVID-19 § Prognosis](63030231.md#Prognosis), and [Long COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_COVID)

The severity of COVID-19 varies. It may take a mild course with few or no symptoms, resembling other common upper respiratory diseases such as the [common cold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_cold). In 3–4% of cases (7.4% for those over age 65) symptoms are severe enough to cause hospitalisation.[^cite_note-pmid33087398-153] Mild cases typically recover within two weeks, while those with severe or critical diseases may take three to six weeks to recover. Among those who have died, the time from symptom onset to death has ranged from two to eight weeks. Prolonged [prothrombin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prothrombin) time and elevated [C-reactive protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C-reactive_protein) levels on admission to the hospital are associated with severe course of COVID-19 and with a transfer to [intensive care units](6332859.md) (ICU).[^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155]

Between 5% and 50% of COVID-19 patients experience [long COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_COVID),[^cite_note-156] a condition characterised by long-term [consequences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sequela) persisting after the typical convalescence period of the disease.[^cite_note-157][^cite_note-158] The most commonly reported clinical presentations are [fatigue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatigue) and memory problems, as well as [malaise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaise), headaches, [shortness of breath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortness_of_breath), loss of smell, [muscle weakness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muscle_weakness), low fever, and [cognitive dysfunction](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_dysfunction).[^cite_note-ref1-159][^cite_note-160][^cite_note-161][^cite_note-162]

## Strategies

Main article: [Public health mitigation of COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health_mitigation_of_COVID-19)



Goals of mitigation include delaying and reducing peak burden on healthcare (*[flattening the curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flattening_the_curve)*) and lessening overall cases and health impact.

[^cite_note-Lancet2020Flatten-163]

[^cite_note-RnW59-164]

&#x20;Moreover, progressively greater increases in healthcare capacity (*[raising the line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raising_the_line)*) such as by increasing bed count, personnel, and equipment, help to meet increased demand.

[^cite_note-Vox_20200407-165]

Many countries attempted to slow or stop the spread of COVID-19 by recommending, mandating, or prohibiting behaviour changes, while others relied primarily on providing information. Measures ranged from public advisories to stringent [lockdowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdowns). [Outbreak control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outbreak_response) strategies are divided into elimination and mitigation. Experts differentiate between elimination strategies (known as "[zero-COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero-COVID)") that aim to completely stop the spread of the virus within the community,[^cite_note-Barcelona-166], and mitigation strategies (commonly known as "[flattening the curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flattening_the_curve)") that attempt to lessen the effects of the virus on society, but which still tolerate some level of transmission within the community.[^cite_note-Livermore-167] These initial strategies can be pursued sequentially or simultaneously during the [acquired immunity phase](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acquired_immunity) through natural and [vaccine-induced immunity](63319438.md).[^cite_note-Bhopal2020-168]

*Nature* reported in 2021 that 90% of researchers who responded to a survey "think that the coronavirus will become [endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic_\(epidemiology\))".[^cite_note-169]

### Containment

Further information: [Zero-COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero-COVID)

Containment is undertaken to stop an outbreak from spreading into the general population. Infected individuals are isolated while they are infectious. People they have interacted with are contacted and isolated for long enough to ensure that they are either not infected or no longer contagious. Screening is the starting point for containment. Screening is done by checking for symptoms to identify infected individuals, who can then be isolated or offered treatment.[^cite_note-f4SpW-170] The zero-COVID strategy involves using public health measures such as [contact tracing](1724851.md), [mass testing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_testing), [border quarantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic), [lockdowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdowns), and [mitigation software](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Use,_and_development_of_software_for_COVID-19_pandemic_mitigation?action=edit\&redlink=1) to stop [community transmission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transmission_of_COVID-19) of COVID-19 as soon as it is detected, with the goal of getting the area back to zero detected infections and resuming normal economic and social activities.[^cite_note-Barcelona-166][^cite_note-Lancet-Li-2020-171] Successful containment or suppression reduces [Rt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_reproduction_number#Effective_reproduction_number) to less than 1.[^cite_note-ImpCollege16mar2020-172]

### Mitigation

Further information: [Flattening the curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flattening_the_curve)

Should containment fail, efforts focus on mitigation: measures taken to slow the spread and limit its effects on the healthcare system and society. Successful mitigation delays and decreases the epidemic peak, known as "flattening the [epidemic curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Epidemic_curve)".[^cite_note-Lancet2020Flatten-163] This decreases the risk of overwhelming health services and provides more time for developing vaccines and treatments.[^cite_note-Lancet2020Flatten-163] Individual behaviour changed in many jurisdictions. Many people [worked from home](158555.md) instead of at their traditional workplaces.[^cite_note-173]

#### Non-pharmaceutical interventions



The CDC and WHO advise that masks (such as worn here by Taiwanese president [Tsai Ing-wen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsai_Ing-wen)) reduce the spread of SARS-CoV-2.

[Non-pharmaceutical interventions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-pharmaceutical_intervention) that may reduce spread include: personal actions such as wearing [face masks](63631542.md), self-quarantine, and [hand hygiene](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hand_washing); community measures aimed at reducing interpersonal contacts such as closing workplaces and schools and cancelling large gatherings; community engagement to encourage acceptance and participation in such interventions; as well as environmental measures such as surface cleaning.[^cite_note-community_mitigation-174]

#### Other measures

More drastic actions, such as quarantining entire populations and strict travel bans have been attempted in various jurisdictions.[^cite_note-RwmYm-175] The [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_government_response_to_COVID-19) and [Australian government approaches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Australia#Responses) have included many lockdowns and are widely considered the most strict. The [New Zealand government response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Zealand_government_response_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic) included the most severe travel restrictions. As part of its [K-Quarantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K-Quarantine) programme, South Korea introduced mass screening and localised quarantines, and issued alerts on the movements of infected individuals. The [Singaporean government's response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Singapore#Government_response) included so-called "[circuit breaker lockdowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_Singapore_circuit_breaker_measures)" and financial support for those affected while also imposing large fines for those who broke quarantine.[^cite_note-shZdf-176]

#### Contact tracing

See also: [Use and development of software for COVID-19 pandemic mitigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Use_and_development_of_software_for_COVID-19_pandemic_mitigation) and [Public health mitigation of COVID-19 § Information technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_health_mitigation_of_COVID-19#Information_technology)

[Contact tracing](1724851.md) attempts to identify recent contacts of newly infected individuals, and to screen them for infection; the traditional approach is to request a list of contacts from infectees, and then telephone or visit the contacts.[^cite_note-web-177] Contact tracing was widely used during the [Western African Ebola virus epidemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_African_Ebola_virus_epidemic) in 2014.[^cite_note-178]

Another approach is to collect location data from mobile devices to identify those who have come in significant contact with infectees, which prompted privacy concerns.[^cite_note-IngramWard20200407-179] On 10 April 2020, Google and [Apple](856.md) announced an initiative for privacy-preserving contact tracing.[^cite_note-0DF25-180][^cite_note-20200410apple-181] In Europe and in the US, [Palantir Technologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palantir_Technologies) initially provided COVID-19 tracking services.[^cite_note-0k3hR-182]

### Healthcare

Further information: [Flattening the curve](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flattening_the_curve), [List of countries by hospital beds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_hospital_beds), and [Shortages related to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

WHO described increasing capacity and adapting healthcare as a fundamental mitigation.[^cite_note-WHOEuropeResponse-183] The ECDC and WHO's European regional office issued guidelines for hospitals and [primary healthcare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primary_health_care) services for shifting resources at multiple levels, including focusing laboratory services toward testing, cancelling elective procedures, separating and isolating patients, and increasing [intensive care](6332859.md) capabilities by training personnel and increasing [ventilators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ventilator) and beds.[^cite_note-WHOEuropeResponse-183][^cite_note-ECDCresponse-184] The pandemic drove widespread adoption of [telehealth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telehealth).[^cite_note-185]

#### Improvised manufacturing



A patient in Ukraine in 2020 wearing a [scuba mask](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diving_mask) in the absence of [artificial ventilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_ventilation)

Due to [supply chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supply_chain) capacity limitations, some manufacturers began [3D printing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3D_printing) material such as nasal swabs and ventilator parts.[^cite_note-technologyreview615420-186][^cite_note-aCPem-187] In one example, an Italian startup received legal threats due to alleged [patent infringement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patent_infringement) after reverse-engineering and printing one hundred requested ventilator valves overnight.[^cite_note-188] Individuals and groups of [makers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maker_culture) created and shared [open source](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Open_source) designs, and manufacturing devices using locally sourced materials, sewing, and 3D printing. Millions of [face shields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Face_shield), protective gowns, and masks were made. Other ad hoc medical supplies included shoe covers, surgical caps, [powered air-purifying respirators](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Powered_air-purifying_respirator), and [hand sanitiser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hand_sanitiser). Novel devices were created such as [ear savers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ear_saver), [non-invasive ventilation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-invasive_ventilation) helmets, and ventilator splitters.[^cite_note-189]

### Herd immunity

In July 2021, several experts expressed concern that achieving [herd immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herd_immunity) may not be possible because Delta can transmit among vaccinated individuals.[^cite_note-\:1-190] CDC published data showing that vaccinated people could transmit Delta, something officials believed was less likely with other variants. Consequently, WHO and CDC encouraged vaccinated people to continue with non-pharmaceutical interventions such as masking, social distancing, and quarantining if exposed.[^cite_note-\:2-191]

## History

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic).

Further information: [Pandemic prevention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandemic_prevention) and [Pandemic predictions and preparations prior to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandemic_predictions_and_preparations_prior_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

### 2019

For a chronological guide, see [Timeline of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_2019).

The [outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Outbreak) was discovered in [Wuhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan) in November 2019. It is possible that human-to-human transmission was happening before the discovery.[^cite_note-Hu2020natureReviews-38][^cite_note-Graham2020immunity-39] Based on a retrospective analysis starting from December 2019, the number of cases in Hubei gradually increased, reaching 60 by 20 December and at least 266 by 31 December.[^cite_note-original_report-192] A [pneumonia](52135.md) cluster was observed on 26 December and treated by Chinese pulmonologist [Zhang Jixian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Jixian). She informed the Wuhan Jianghan CDC on 27 December.[^cite_note-D54YB-193] After analysing pneumonia patient samples, a genetic sequencing company named Vision Medicals reported the discovery of a [novel coronavirus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novel_coronavirus) to the [China CDC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Center_for_Disease_Control_and_Prevention) (CCDC) on 28 December.[^cite_note-AP_OR-194][^cite_note-caixintrace-195] Two days later, a test report from CapitalBio Medlab addressed to the [Wuhan Central Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Hospital_of_Wuhan) reported an erroneous positive result for [SARS](196741.md), causing doctors there to alert authorities. Eight of those doctors, including ophthalmologist [Li Wenliang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Li_Wenliang) (1985–2020), were detained by police on 3 January for "spreading false rumours".[^cite_note-siStf-196] That evening, Wuhan Municipal Health Commission (WMHC) issued a notice about "the treatment of pneumonia of unknown cause".[^cite_note-promedmail6864153-197] The next day, WMHC made the announcement public, confirming 27 cases[^cite_note-AutoDW-69-198][^cite_note-AutoDW-68-199]—enough to trigger an investigation.[^cite_note-bbc50984025-200] On 31 December, the WHO office in China was notified about the cluster of unknown pneumonia cases[^cite_note-WHO_report-22][^cite_note-AutoDW-69-198] and immediately launched an investigation.[^cite_note-bbc50984025-200]

Official Chinese sources claimed that the early cases were mostly linked to the [Huanan Seafood Wholesale Market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Huanan_Seafood_Wholesale_Market), which also sold live animals.[^cite_note-characteristicsZH-201] In May 2020, CCDC director George Gao initially ruled out the market as a possible origin, as animal samples collected there had tested negative.[^cite_note-ALmAQ-202]

### 2020

Timelines of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020 by month: [January](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_January_2020), [February](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_February_2020), [March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_March_2020), [April](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_April_2020), [May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_May_2020), [June](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_June_2020), [July](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_July_2020), [August](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_August_2020), [September](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_September_2020), [October](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_October_2020), [November](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_November_2020), [December](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_December_2020)



A highway sign discouraging travel in [Toronto](64646.md), March 2020

On 11 January, WHO was notified by the Chinese National Health Commission that the outbreak was associated with exposures in the market, and that China had identified a new type of [coronavirus](201983.md), which it isolated on 7 January.[^cite_note-WHO_report-22]

Initially, the number of cases doubled approximately every seven and a half days.[^cite_note-Qun29Jan2020-203] In early and mid-January, the virus spread to other [Chinese provinces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provinces_of_China), helped by the [Chinese New Year migration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chunyun). Wuhan was a transport hub and major rail interchange.[^cite_note-WHO_report_28_February_2020-204] On 10 January, the virus' genome was shared publicly.[^cite_note-205] A retrospective study published in March found that 6,174 people had reported symptoms by 20 January.[^cite_note-Epidemiology17Feb2020-206] A 24 January report indicated human transmission was likely occurring, and recommended [personal protective equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_protective_equipment) for health workers. It also advocated testing, given the outbreak's "pandemic potential".[^cite_note-Huang24Jan2020-207][^cite_note-Horton_18_March-208] On 30 January, 7,818 infections had been confirmed, leading WHO to declare the outbreak a [Public Health Emergency of International Concern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Health_Emergency_of_International_Concern) (PHEIC),[^cite_note-WHO_PHEICSR-209][^cite_note-WHO_PHEIC_decl2-210][^cite_note-211] upgrading it to a pandemic on 11 March.[^cite_note-start-3] On 31 January, the first published modelling study warned of inevitable "independent self-sustaining outbreaks in major cities globally" and called for "large-scale public health interventions".[^cite_note-pmid32014114-212]

By 31 January, Italy indicated its first confirmed infections had occurred, in two tourists from China.[^cite_note-Corriere_20Jan-213] On 19 March, Italy overtook China as the country with the most reported deaths.[^cite_note-sky11960412-214]

By 26 March, the United States had overtaken China and Italy as the country with the highest number of confirmed infections.[^cite_note-NYT-20200326-215] Genomic analysis indicated that the majority of [New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_New_York_\(state\))'s confirmed infections came from Europe, rather than directly from Asia.[^cite_note-20200408nytimes-216] Testing of prior samples revealed a person who was infected in France on 27 December 2019[^cite_note-France-retest-217][^cite_note-Deslandes_2020-218] and a person in the United States who died from the disease on 6 February.[^cite_note-PBS-2wks-219]



An exhausted [anaesthesiologist](417068.md) in [Pesaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pesaro), Italy, 19 March 2020

In October, WHO reported that one in ten people around the world may have been infected, or 780 million people, while only 35 million infections had been confirmed.[^cite_note-bbc_oneinten-220]

On 9 November, [Pfizer](62304.md) released trial results for a candidate vaccine, showing a 90 per cent effectiveness in preventing infection. That day, [Novavax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Novavax) submitted an [FDA Fast Track](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast_track_\(FDA\)) application for their vaccine.[^cite_note-FEv4C-221][^cite_note-NPZ5P-222] On 14 December, [Public Health England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Health_England) reported that a variant had been discovered in the UK's southeast, predominantly in [Kent](16766.md). The variant, later named [Alpha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variant_of_Concern_202012/01), showed changes to the [spike protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronavirus_spike_protein) that could make the virus more infectious. As of 13 December, 1,108 infections had been confirmed in the UK.[^cite_note-223][^cite_note-224] On 4 February 2020, US Secretary of Health and Human Services [Alex Azar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Azar) waived liability for vaccine manufacturers in all cases except those involving "willful misconduct".[^cite_note-mXTUz-225][^cite_note-226]

### 2021

Timelines of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2021 by month: [January](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_January_2021), [February](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_February_2021), [March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_March_2021), [April](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_April_2021), [May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_May_2021), [June](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_June_2021), [July](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_July_2021), [August](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_August_2021), [September](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_September_2021), [October](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_October_2021), [November](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_November_2021), [December](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_December_2021)

On 2 January, the [Alpha variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Alpha_variant), first discovered in the UK, had been identified in 33 countries.[^cite_note-227] On 6 January, the [Gamma variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Gamma_variant) was first identified in Japanese travellers returning from Brazil.[^cite_note-228] On 29 January, it was reported that the Novavax vaccine was 49 per cent effective against the [Beta variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Beta_variant) in a clinical trial in South Africa.[^cite_note-229][^cite_note-230] The [CoronaVac vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CoronaVac_vaccine) was reported to be 50.4 per cent effective in a clinical trial in Brazil.[^cite_note-231]



A temporary hospital for COVID-19 patients in [Santo André](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Santo_Andr%C3%A9,_S%C3%A3o_Paulo), Brazil, in March 2021

On 12 March, several countries stopped using the [Oxford–AstraZeneca COVID-19 vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford%E2%80%93AstraZeneca_COVID-19_vaccine) due to blood clotting problems, specifically [cerebral venous sinus thrombosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cerebral_venous_sinus_thrombosis) (CVST).[^cite_note-232] On 20 March, the WHO and European Medicines Agency found no link to [thrombosis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thrombosis), leading several countries to resume administering the vaccine.[^cite_note-233] In March, WHO reported that an animal host was the most likely origin, without ruling out other possibilities.[^cite_note-who-origins-20210330-2][^cite_note-Maxmen2021whoReport-37] The [Delta variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Delta_variant) was first identified in India. In mid-April, the variant was first detected in the UK and two months later it had become a full-fledged third wave in the country, forcing the government to delay reopening that was originally scheduled for June.[^cite_note-234] On 10 November, Germany advised against the [Moderna vaccine](63399660.md) for people under 30, due to a possible association with [myocarditis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myocarditis).[^cite_note-235] On 24 November, the [Omicron variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant) was detected in South Africa; a few days later the WHO declared it a VoC (variant of concern).[^cite_note-236] The new variant is more infectious than the Delta variant.[^cite_note-237]

### 2022

Timelines of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2022 by month: [January](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_January_2022), [February](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_February_2022), [March](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_March_2022), [April](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_April_2022), [May](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_May_2022), [June](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_June_2022), [July](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_July_2022), [August](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_August_2022), [September](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_September_2022), [October](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_October_2022), [November](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_November_2022), [December](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_December_2022)



Scanning electron micrograph (colorised) of cell infected with the Omicron strain of SARS-CoV-2 virus particles *green*

On 1 January, Europe passed 100 million cases amid a surge in the [Omicron variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant).[^cite_note-238] Later that month, the WHO recommended the [rheumatoid arthritis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rheumatoid_arthritis) drug [baricitinib](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baricitinib) for severe or critical patients. It also recommended the monoclonal antibody [sotrovimab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sotrovimab) in patients with non-severe disease, but only those who are at highest risk of hospitalisation.[^cite_note-239]

On 24 January, the [Institute for Health Metrics and Evaluation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Health_Metrics_and_Evaluation) estimated that about 57% of the world's population had been infected by COVID-19.[^cite_note-57percent1-63][^cite_note-57percent2-64] By 6 March, it was reported that the total worldwide death count had surpassed 6 million people.[^cite_note-240] By 6 July, Omicron subvariants [BA.4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BA.4) and [BA.5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BA.5) had spread worldwide.[^cite_note-241] WHO Director-General [Tedros Ghebreyesus](39712205.md) stated on 14 September 2022, that "\[The world has] never been in a better position to end the pandemic", citing the lowest number of weekly reported deaths since March 2020. He continued, "We are not there yet. But the end is in sight—we can see the finish line".[^cite_note-242][^cite_note-243][^cite_note-244][^cite_note-245]

On 21 October, the United States surpassed 99 million cases of COVID-19, the most cases of any country.[^cite_note-246] By 30 October, the worldwide daily death toll was 424, the lowest since 385 deaths were reported on 12 March 2020.[^cite_note-247] 17 November marked the three-year anniversary since health officials in China first detected COVID-19.[^cite_note-248]

On 11 November, the WHO reported that deaths since the month of February had dropped 90 per cent. Director-General Tedros said this was "cause for optimism."[^cite_note-249] On 3 December, the WHO indicated that, "at least 90% of the world's population has some level of immunity to Sars-CoV-2."[^cite_note-250] In early December, China began lifting some of its most stringent lockdown measures. Subsequent data from China's health authorities revealed that 248 million people, nearly 18 per cent of its population, had been infected in the first 20 days of that month.[^cite_note-251] On 29 December, the US joined Italy, Japan, Taiwan, and India in requiring negative COVID-19 test results from all people travelling from China due to the new surge in cases. The EU refused similar measures, stating that the BF7 omicron variant had already spread throughout Europe without becoming dominant.[^cite_note-252][^cite_note-253]

### 2023

[Timeline of the COVID-19 pandemic in 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_2023)

On 4 January 2023, the WHO said the information shared by China during the recent surge in infections lacked data, such as hospitalisation rates.[^cite_note-254] On 10 January, the WHO's Europe office said the recent viral surge in China posed "no immediate threat."[^cite_note-255] On 16 January, the WHO recommended that China monitor excess mortality to provide "a more comprehensive understanding of the impact of COVID-19".[^cite_note-256]

On 30 January, the three-year anniversary of the original declaration, the WHO determined that COVID-19 still met the criteria for a public health emergency of international concern (PHEIC).[^cite_note-257]

On 19 March, WHO Director-General Tedros indicated he was "confident" the COVID-19 pandemic would cease to be a public health emergency by the end of the year.[^cite_note-258] On 5 May, the WHO downgraded COVID-19 from being a global health emergency, though it continued to refer to it as a pandemic.[^cite_note-259] The WHO does not make official declarations of when pandemics end.[^cite_note-reuters-4][^cite_note-guardian-260] The decision came after Tedros convened with the International Health Regulations Emergency Committee, wherein the Committee noted that due to the decrease in deaths and hospitalisations, and the prevalence of vaccinations and the level of general immunity, it was time to remove the emergency designation and "transition to long-term management".[^cite_note-WHO_Statement_5_May_2023-261] Tedros agreed, and the WHO reduced the classification to an "established and ongoing health issue".[^cite_note-WHO_Statement_5_May_2023-261] In a press conference, Tedros remarked that the diminishing threat from COVID-19 had "allowed most countries to return to life as we knew it before COVID-19."[^cite_note-npr-262]

In September the WHO said it had observed "concerning" trends in COVID-19 case numbers and hospitalisations, although analysis was hampered because many countries were no longer recording COVID-19 case statistics.[^cite_note-263]

In November 2023, in response to viral mutations and changing characteristics of infection, the WHO adjusted its treatment guidelines. Among other changes, remdesivir and molnupiravir were now recommended only for the most severe cases, and [deuremidevir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deuremidevir) and ivermectin were recommended against.[^cite_note-264]

### 2024

In March 2024, amid fewer hospitalizations and deaths from COVID-19, CDC released updated recommendations on protection from it.[^cite_note-265] As the virus continued to evolve in its [spike protein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spike_protein), in April WHO updated its recommendation on vaccine [antigen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antigen) composition to enhance vaccine-induced immune responses. It advised a monovalent [JN.1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JN.1) lineage as the antigen in future COVID-19 vaccines.[^cite_note-266]

### 2025

In mid-2025, WHO lowered the global public health risk from COVID-19 from high to moderate due to high [population immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Population_immunity) and improved clinical management. However, WHO advised continued vigilance due to ongoing circulation and virus evolution in humans and known [animal reservoirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_reservoir), low vaccine uptake, and insufficient genomic surveillance data.[^cite_note-267] In May, World Health Assembly adopted the [Pandemic Agreement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandemic_Agreement) that was spurred by deficiencies in global COVID response.[^cite_note-268] In June, the WHO scientific panel published a report on COVID origin, concluding that "the weight of available evidence…suggests zoonotic spillover... either directly from bats or through an intermediate host". In the same year, updated [International Health Regulations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Health_Regulations) entered into force, spurred by the response to the COVID pandemic.[^cite_note-269]

## Responses

Main articles: [COVID-19 lockdowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdowns), [COVID-19 pandemic by country and territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_by_country_and_territory), and [National responses to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_responses_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

National reactions ranged from strict lockdowns to public education campaigns.[^cite_note-IVdfl-270] WHO recommended that curfews and lockdowns should be short-term measures to reorganise, regroup, rebalance resources, and protect the health care system.[^cite_note-58tIM-271] As of 26 March 2020, 1.7 billion people worldwide were under some form of lockdown.[^cite_note-theguardian500000-272] This increased to 3.9 billion people by the first week of April—more than half the [world's population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_population).[^cite_note-20200403euronews-273][^cite_note-20200328businessinsider-274]

In several countries, [protests rose against restrictions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_against_responses_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic) such as lockdowns. A February 2021 study found that protests against restrictions were likely to directly increase the spread of the virus.[^cite_note-275]

### Asia

Main article: [COVID-19 pandemic in Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Asia)

As of the end of 2021, Asia's peak had come at the same time and at the same level as the world as a whole, in May 2021.[^cite_note-276] However, cumulatively they had experienced only half of the global average in cases.[^cite_note-277]



A [temporary hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fangcang_Hospital) constructed in Wuhan in February 2020

China opted for containment, instituting strict lockdowns to eliminate viral spread.[^cite_note-\:02-278][^cite_note-279] The vaccines distributed in China included the [BIBP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinopharm_BIBP_COVID-19_vaccine), [WIBP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinopharm_WIBP_COVID-19_vaccine), and [CoronaVac](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CoronaVac).[^cite_note-280] It was reported on 11 December 2021, that China had vaccinated 1.162 billion of its citizens, or 82.5% of the total population of the country against COVID-19.[^cite_note-281] China's large-scale adoption of [zero-COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero-COVID) had largely contained the first waves of infections of the disease.[^cite_note-\:02-278][^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283] When the waves of infections due to the [Omicron variant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_Omicron_variant) followed, China was almost alone in pursuing the strategy of zero-Covid to combat the spread of the virus in 2022.[^cite_note-284] Lockdown continued to be employed in November to combat a new wave of cases;[^cite_note-285][^cite_note-286] however, [protests erupted in cities across China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_COVID-19_protests_in_China) over the country's stringent measures,[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] and in December that year, the country relaxed its zero-COVID policy.[^cite_note-289] On 20 December 2022, the Chinese State Council narrowed its definition of what would be counted as a COVID-19 death to include solely respiratory failure, which led to scepticism by health experts of the government's total death count[^cite_note-290][^cite_note-291] at a time when hospitals reported being overwhelmed with cases following the abrupt discontinuation of zero-COVID.[^cite_note-292]

The first case in India was reported on 30 January 2020. India ordered a nationwide lockdown starting 24 March 2020,[^cite_note-mrGsK-293] with a phased unlock beginning 1 June 2020. Six cities accounted for around half of reported cases—[Mumbai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Maharashtra), [Delhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Delhi), [Ahmedabad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Gujarat), [Chennai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Tamil_Nadu), [Pune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Maharashtra) and [Kolkata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_West_Bengal).[^cite_note-sHMHJ-294] Post-lockdown, the Government of India introduced a contact tracking app called [Aarogya Setu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aarogya_Setu) to help authorities manage contact tracing and vaccine distribution.[^cite_note-295] India's vaccination programme was considered to be the world's largest and most successful with over 90% of citizens getting the first dose and another 65% getting the second dose.[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297] A second wave hit India in April 2021, straining healthcare services.[^cite_note-298] On 21 October 2021, it was reported that the country had surpassed 1 billion vaccinations.[^cite_note-299]



Disinfection of [Tehran Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tehran_Metro) trains to prevent COVID-19 transmission. Similar measures have also been taken in other countries.

[^cite_note-2kQpo-300]

Iran reported its first confirmed cases on 19 February 2020, in [Qom](638742.md).[^cite_note-wHBRB-301][^cite_note-4yrqM-302] Early measures included the cancellation/closure of concerts and other cultural events,[^cite_note-Xm5nq-303] Friday prayers,[^cite_note-Urdn6-304] and school and university campuses.[^cite_note-2YX6a-305] Iran became a centre of the pandemic in February 2020.[^cite_note-HHOnl-306][^cite_note-New_Yorker-307] More than ten countries had traced their outbreaks to Iran by 28 February, indicating a more severe outbreak than the 388 reported cases.[^cite_note-New_Yorker-307][^cite_note-D2Xpc-308] The [Iranian Parliament](383276.md) closed, after 23 of its 290 members tested positive on 3 March 2020.[^cite_note-fXv2K-309] At least twelve sitting or former Iranian politicians and government officials had died by 17 March 2020.[^cite_note-oxtUC-310] By August 2021, the pandemic's fifth wave peaked, with more than 400 deaths in 1 day.[^cite_note-311]

COVID-19 was confirmed in South Korea on 20 January 2020. Military bases were quarantined after tests showed three infected soldiers.[^cite_note-b82186-312] South Korea introduced what was then considered the world's largest and best-organised screening programme, isolating infected people, and tracing and quarantining contacts.[^cite_note-science20200317-313] Screening methods included mandatory self-reporting by new international arrivals through mobile application,[^cite_note-nbcnews1167376-314] combined with [drive-through](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drive-through) testing,[^cite_note-CvyZS-315] and increasing testing capability to 20,000 people/day.[^cite_note-fdhQW-316] Despite some early criticisms,[^cite_note-imx38-317] South Korea's programme was considered a success in controlling the outbreak without quarantining entire cities.[^cite_note-science20200317-313][^cite_note-Wypis-318][^cite_note-joins23778577-319]

### Europe

Main article: [COVID-19 pandemic in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Europe)



Deaths per 100,000 residents

The COVID-19 pandemic arrived in Europe with its first confirmed case in [Bordeaux](4097.md), [France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_France), on 24 January 2020, and subsequently spread widely across the continent. By 17 March 2020, every country in Europe had confirmed a case,[^cite_note-320] and all had reported at least one death, with the exception of [Vatican City](63299650.md). Italy was the first European nation to experience a major outbreak in early 2020, becoming the first country worldwide to introduce a national [lockdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdowns_in_Italy).[^cite_note-321] By 13 March 2020, the WHO declared Europe the epicentre of the pandemic[^cite_note-322][^cite_note-323] and it remained so until the WHO announced it had been overtaken by [South America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_South_America) on 22 May.[^cite_note-324] By 18 March 2020, more than 250 million people were in [lockdown](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stay-at-home_order) in Europe.[^cite_note-325] Despite [deployment of COVID-19 vaccines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deployment_of_COVID-19_vaccines), Europe became the pandemic's epicentre once again in late 2021.[^cite_note-326][^cite_note-327]

The [Italian outbreak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Italy) began on 31 January 2020, when two Chinese tourists tested positive for SARS-CoV-2 in Rome.[^cite_note-Corriere_20Jan-213] Cases began to rise sharply, which prompted the government to suspend flights to and from China and declare a state of emergency.[^cite_note-thelocal-flight-328] On 22 February 2020, the Council of Ministers announced a new decree-law to contain the outbreak, which quarantined more than 50,000 people in northern Italy.[^cite_note-AutoDW-201-329] On 4 March, the Italian government ordered schools and universities closed as Italy reached a hundred deaths.
