Sport was suspended completely for at least one month.[^cite_note-Kv4Ld-330] On 11 March, Italian Prime Minister Giuseppe Conte closed down nearly all commercial activity except supermarkets and pharmacies.[^cite_note-To26Y-331][^cite_note-ClzT6-332] On 19 April, the first wave ebbed, as 7-day deaths declined to 433.[^cite_note-thehill493586-333] On 13 October, the Italian government again issued restrictive rules to contain the second wave.[^cite_note-KhaW7-334] On 10 November, Italy surpassed 1 million confirmed infections.[^cite_note-8XEwk-335] On 23 November, it was reported that the second wave of the virus had led some hospitals to stop accepting patients.[^cite_note-336]

Elderly woman rolls up sleeve as two nurses administer a vaccine.

Vaccinations at a [retirement home](1563666.md) in [Gijón](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gij%C3%B3n), Spain on 12 December 2020

The virus was first confirmed to have spread to Spain on 31 January 2020, when a German tourist tested positive for SARS-CoV-2 on [La Gomera](220016.md) in the Canary Islands.[^cite_note-SanidadConfirmaEnLaGomera-337] Post-hoc genetic analysis has shown that at least 15 strains of the virus had been imported, and [community transmission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_transmission) began by mid-February.[^cite_note-genetic_analysis-338] On 29 March, it was announced that, beginning the following day, all non-essential workers were ordered to remain at home for the next 14 days.[^cite_note-339] The number of cases increased again in July in a number of cities including [Barcelona](4443.md), [Zaragoza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaragoza), and [Madrid](41188263.md), which led to reimposition of some restrictions but no national lockdown.[^cite_note-bbc25july-340][^cite_note-elpais6aug-341][^cite_note-telegraph_aug16-342][^cite_note-343] By September 2021, Spain was one of the countries with the highest percentage of its population vaccinated (76% fully vaccinated and 79% with the first dose).[^cite_note-auto2-344] Italy is ranked second at 75%.[^cite_note-auto2-344]

Sweden differed from most other European countries in that it mostly remained open.[^cite_note-NYT20200515-345] Per the [Swedish constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_constitution), the [Public Health Agency of Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Health_Agency_of_Sweden) has autonomy that prevents political interference and the agency favoured remaining open. The Swedish strategy focused on longer-term measures, based on the assumption that after lockdown the virus would resume spreading, with the same result.[^cite_note-7ys2k-346][^cite_note-I06mN-347] By the end of June, Sweden no longer had [excess mortality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Excess_mortality).[^cite_note-HEUrz-348]

[Devolution in the United Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Devolution_in_the_United_Kingdom) meant that each of its four [countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Countries_of_the_United_Kingdom) developed its own response. England's restrictions were shorter-lived than the others.[^cite_note-8VnMf-349] The [UK government](25318118.md) started enforcing social distancing and quarantine measures on 18 March 2020.[^cite_note-PkWGD-350][^cite_note-Pkx25-351] On 16 March, Prime Minister [Boris Johnson](19065069.md) advised against non-essential travel and social contact, praising [work from home](158555.md) and avoiding venues such as pubs, restaurants, and theatres.[^cite_note-5Q93q-352][^cite_note-hYdUy-353] On 20 March, the government ordered all leisure establishments to close,[^cite_note-dmBNt-354] and promised to prevent unemployment.[^cite_note-mV2p3-355] On 23 March, Johnson banned gatherings and restricted non-essential travel and outdoor activity. Unlike previous measures, these restrictions were enforceable by police through fines and dispersal of gatherings. Most non-essential businesses were ordered to close.[^cite_note-bbc-pm-2403-356] On 24 April 2020, it was reported that a promising vaccine trial had begun in England; the government pledged more than £50 million toward research.[^cite_note-vKeXC-357] On 16 April 2020, it was reported that the UK would have first access to the Oxford vaccine, due to a prior contract; should the trial be successful, some 30 million doses would be available.[^cite_note-Lbt4o-358] On 2 December 2020, the UK became the first developed country to approve the Pfizer vaccine; 800,000 doses were immediately available for use.[^cite_note-359] In August 2022 it was reported that viral infection cases had declined in the UK.[^cite_note-360]

### North America

Main article: [COVID-19 pandemic in North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_North_America)

The virus arrived in the [United States](63136490.md) on 13 January 2020.[^cite_note-361] Cases were reported in all North American countries after [Saint Kitts and Nevis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Saint_Kitts_and_Nevis) confirmed a case on 25 March, and in all North American territories after [Bonaire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Bonaire) confirmed a case on 16 April.[^cite_note-FirstBonaire_local-362]



The hospital ship [USNS *Comfort*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USNS_Comfort) arrives in [Manhattan](45470.md) on 30 March 2020.

Per *Our World in Data*, 103,436,829[^cite_note-Template\:COVID-19_data-5] confirmed cases have been reported in the United States with 1,239,049[^cite_note-Template\:COVID-19_data-5] deaths, the most of any country, and [the nineteenth-highest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_death_rates_by_country) per capita worldwide.[^cite_note-JH_Mortality-363] COVID-19 is the [deadliest pandemic in US history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_disasters_in_the_United_States_by_death_toll);[^cite_note-364] it was the third-leading cause of death in the US in 2020, behind heart disease and cancer.[^cite_note-365] From 2019 to 2020, US life expectancy dropped by 3 years for Hispanic Americans, 2.9 years for African Americans, and 1.2 years for white Americans.[^cite_note-Bosman-366] These effects have persisted as US deaths due to COVID-19 in 2021 exceeded those in 2020.[^cite_note-367] In the United States, COVID-19 vaccines became available under emergency use in December 2020, beginning the [national vaccination programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccination_in_the_United_States). The first COVID-19 vaccine was officially approved by the [Food and Drug Administration](11632.md) on 23 August 2021.[^cite_note-368] By 18 November 2022, while cases in the U.S. had declined, COVID variants BQ.1/BQ.1.1 had become dominant in the country.[^cite_note-369][^cite_note-370]

In March 2020, as cases of community transmission were confirmed across [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Canada), all of its provinces and territories declared states of emergency. Provinces and territories, to varying degrees, implemented school and daycare closures, prohibitions on gatherings, closures of non-essential businesses, and restrictions on entry. Canada severely restricted its border access, barring travellers from all countries with some exceptions.[^cite_note-371] Cases surged across Canada, notably in the provinces of [British Columbia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_British_Columbia), [Alberta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Alberta), [Quebec](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Quebec) and [Ontario](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Ontario), with the formation of the [Atlantic Bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atlantic_Bubble), a [travel-restricted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_restrictions_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic) area of the country (formed of the four [Atlantic provinces](71095.md)).[^cite_note-372] Vaccine passports were adopted in all provinces and two of the territories.[^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374] Per a report on 11 November 2022, Canadian health authorities saw a surge in influenza, while COVID-19 was expected to rise during winter.[^cite_note-375]

### South America

Main article: [COVID-19 pandemic in South America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_South_America)



Mass graves being prepared for COVID-19 victims in [Cochabamba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cochabamba), Bolivia

The COVID-19 pandemic was confirmed to have reached South America on 26 February 2020, when Brazil confirmed a case in [São Paulo](390875.md).[^cite_note-376] By 3 April, all countries and territories in South America had recorded at least one case.[^cite_note-Stuff/Fairfax-377] On 13 May 2020, it was reported that Latin America and the [Caribbean](18956035.md) had reported over 400,000 cases of COVID-19 infection with 23,091 deaths. On 22 May 2020, citing the rapid increase of [infections in Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Brazil), the WHO declared South America the epicentre of the pandemic.[^cite_note-378][^cite_note-379] As of 16 July 2021, South America had recorded 34,359,631 confirmed cases and 1,047,229 deaths from COVID-19. Due to a shortage of testing and medical facilities, it is believed that the outbreak is far larger than the official numbers show.[^cite_note-380]

The virus was confirmed to have spread to Brazil on 25 February 2020,[^cite_note-381] when a man from [São Paulo](390875.md) who had travelled to Italy tested positive for the virus.[^cite_note-382] The disease had spread to every [federative unit of Brazil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federative_units_of_Brazil) by 21 March. On 19 June 2020, the country reported its one millionth case and nearly 49,000 reported deaths.[^cite_note-383][^cite_note-384] One estimate of [under-reporting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Under-reporting#Disease) was 22.62% of total reported COVID-19 mortality in 2020.[^cite_note-385][^cite_note-386][^cite_note-387] As of 18 September 2026, Brazil, with 38,050,567[^cite_note-Template\:COVID-19_data-5] confirmed cases and 704,066[^cite_note-Template\:COVID-19_data-5] deaths, has the third-highest number of confirmed cases and second-highest death toll from COVID-19 in the world, behind only [those of the United States](63136490.md) and [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_India).[^cite_note-388]

### Africa

Main article: [COVID-19 pandemic in Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Africa)



[US Air Force](32090.md) personnel unload a [C-17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_C-17_Globemaster_III) aircraft carrying medical supplies in [Niamey](57061.md), Niger, in April 2020.

The COVID-19 pandemic was confirmed to have spread to Africa on 14 February 2020, with the first confirmed case announced in [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Egypt).[^cite_note-bbc_51509248-389][^cite_note-390] The first confirmed case in [sub-Saharan Africa](27067.md) was announced in [Nigeria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Nigeria) at the end of February 2020.[^cite_note-bbc_51671834-391] Within three months, the virus had spread throughout the continent; [Lesotho](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Lesotho), the last African sovereign state to have remained free of the virus, reported its first case on 13 May 2020.[^cite_note-reuters_idUSKBN22P1R4-392][^cite_note-latimes.com-393] By 26 May, it appeared that most African countries were experiencing community transmission, although testing capacity was limited.[^cite_note-theguardian_20200526_africa-394] Most of the identified imported cases arrived from Europe and the United States rather than from China where the virus originated.[^cite_note-Africa_Braces-395] Many preventive measures [were implemented](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_responses_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic_in_Africa) by different countries in Africa including travel restrictions, flight cancellations, and event cancellations.[^cite_note-396] Despite fears, Africa reported lower death rates than other, more economically developed regions.[^cite_note-NYT_Africa_deaths-397]

In early June 2021, Africa faced a third wave of COVID infections with cases rising in 14 countries.[^cite_note-theguardian_20210607_third-398] By 4 July the continent recorded more than 251,000 new COVID cases, a 20% increase from the prior week and a 12% increase from the January peak. More than sixteen African countries, including [Malawi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Malawi) and [Senegal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Senegal), recorded an uptick in new cases.[^cite_note-Mendez-399] The WHO labelled it Africa's 'Worst Pandemic Week Ever'.[^cite_note-400] In October 2022, WHO reported that most countries on the African continent will miss the goal of 70 per cent vaccination by the end of 2022.[^cite_note-401]

### Oceania

Main article: [COVID-19 pandemic in Oceania](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Oceania)



Empty shelves at a [Coles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coles_Supermarkets) grocery store in [Brisbane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brisbane), Australia, in April 2020

The COVID-19 pandemic was confirmed to have reached Oceania on 25 January 2020, with the first confirmed case reported in [Melbourne](17306237.md), [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Australia).[^cite_note-AustraliaCase1-402][^cite_note-Brisbane2021-403] It has since spread elsewhere in the region.[^cite_note-WHO_Dashboard-404][^cite_note-Brisbane2021-403] Australia and [New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_New_Zealand) were praised for their handling of the pandemic in comparison to other Western nations, with New Zealand and each state in Australia wiping out all community transmission of the virus several times even after re-introduction into the community.[^cite_note-reuters-oz-10days-405][^cite_note-bloomberg-oz-tassie-price-406][^cite_note-407]

As a result of the high transmissibility of the Delta variant, however, by August 2021, the Australian states of [New South Wales](21654.md) and [Victoria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victoria_\(Australia\)) had conceded defeat in their eradication efforts.[^cite_note-408] In early October 2021, New Zealand also abandoned its elimination strategy.[^cite_note-The_Guardian_NZ_abandons_elimination-409] In November and December, following vaccination efforts, the remaining states of Australia, excluding Western Australia, voluntarily gave up [COVID-Zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zero-COVID) to open up state and international borders.[^cite_note-410][^cite_note-411][^cite_note-412] The open borders allowed the Omicron variant of COVID-19 to enter quickly, and cases subsequently exceeded 120,000 a day.[^cite_note-413] By early March 2022, with cases exceeding 1,000 a day, Western Australia conceded defeat in its eradication strategy and opened its borders.[^cite_note-414] Despite record cases, Australian jurisdictions slowly removed restrictions such as close contact isolation, mask wearing, and density limits by April 2022.[^cite_note-415]

On 9 September 2022, restrictions were significantly relaxed. The aircraft mask mandate was scrapped nationwide, and daily reporting transitioned to weekly reporting.[^cite_note-416][^cite_note-417][^cite_note-418] On 14 September, COVID-19 disaster payment for isolating persons was extended for mandatory isolation.[^cite_note-419] By 22 September, all states had ended mask mandates on public transport, including in Victoria, where the mandate had lasted for approximately 800 days.[^cite_note-420] On 30 September 2022, all Australian leaders declared the emergency response finished and announced the end of isolation requirements. These changes were due in part to high levels of 'hybrid immunity' and low case numbers.[^cite_note-421]

### Antarctica

Main article: [COVID-19 pandemic in Antarctica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Antarctica)

Due to its remoteness and sparse population, [Antarctica](18959138.md) was the last continent to have confirmed cases of COVID-19.[^cite_note-422][^cite_note-423][^cite_note-424] The first cases were reported in December 2020, almost a year after the first cases of COVID-19 were detected in China. At least 36 people were infected in the first outbreak in 2020,[^cite_note-425] with several other outbreaks taking place in 2021 and 2022.[^cite_note-426]

### United Nations

Main article: [United Nations response to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_response_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

The [United Nations Conference on Trade and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Conference_on_Trade_and_Development) (UNSC) was criticised for its slow response, especially regarding the UN's [global ceasefire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_ceasefire), which aimed to open up humanitarian access to conflict zones.[^cite_note-\:22-427][^cite_note-Krm4a-428] The United Nations Security Council was criticised due to the inadequate manner in which it dealt with the COVID-19 pandemic, namely the poor ability to create international collaboration during this crisis.[^cite_note-429][^cite_note-430]

On 23 March 2020, United Nations Secretary-General [António Manuel de Oliveira Guterres](471806.md) appealed for a global [ceasefire](592591.md);[^cite_note-tFb4N-431][^cite_note-lzoeV-432] 172 UN member states and observers signed a non-binding supporting statement in June,[^cite_note-AGhvz-433] and the [UN Security Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_Security_Council) passed a [resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Security_Council_Resolution_2532) supporting it in July.[^cite_note-9rN79-434][^cite_note-nJTuX-435]

On 29 September 2020, Guterres urged the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md) to help certain countries via debt relief and also call for countries to increase contributions to develop vaccines.[^cite_note-436]

### WHO

Main article: [World Health Organization's response to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Health_Organization's_response_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)



[World Health Organization](33583.md) representatives holding joint meeting with [Tehran](57654.md) city administrators in March 2020

The WHO spearheaded initiatives such as the [COVID-19 Solidarity Response Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_Solidarity_Response_Fund) to raise money for the pandemic response, the [UN COVID-19 Supply Chain Task Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UN_COVID-19_Supply_Chain_Task_Force), and the [solidarity trial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Solidarity_trial) for investigating potential treatment options for the disease. The [COVAX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVAX) programme, co-led by the WHO, [GAVI](38346167.md), and the [Coalition for Epidemic Preparedness Innovations](51522180.md) (CEPI), aimed to accelerate the development, manufacture, and distribution of COVID-19 vaccines, and to guarantee fair and equitable access across the world.[^cite_note-437][^cite_note-438]

## Restrictions

Further information: [Timeline of the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic) and [International aid related to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_aid_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

Workers unloading boxes of medical supplies at Villamor Air Base.

Donated medical supplies received in the Philippines

The pandemic shook the world's economy, with especially severe economic damage in the United States, Europe, and Latin America.[^cite_note-439][^cite_note-Effect_of_COVID-19_on_food_security-440] A consensus report by American intelligence agencies in April 2021 concluded, "Efforts to contain and manage the virus have reinforced nationalist trends globally, as some states turned inward to protect their citizens and sometimes cast blame on marginalised groups". COVID-19 inflamed partisanship and polarisation around the world as bitter arguments exploded over how to respond. International trade was disrupted amid the formation of no-entry enclaves.[^cite_note-441]

### Travel restrictions

Main article: [Travel restrictions related to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travel_restrictions_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

The pandemic led many countries and regions to impose quarantines, entry bans, or other restrictions, either for citizens, recent travellers to affected areas,[^cite_note-20200326nytimes-442] or for all travellers.[^cite_note-443][^cite_note-444] Travel collapsed worldwide, damaging the travel sector. The effectiveness of travel restrictions was questioned as the virus spread across the world.[^cite_note-20200224nationalgeographic-445] One study found that travel restrictions only modestly affected the initial spread, unless combined with other [infection prevention and control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infection_prevention_and_control) measures.[^cite_note-DJYtB-446][^cite_note-447] Researchers concluded that "travel restrictions are most useful in the early and late phase of an epidemic" and "restrictions of travel from Wuhan unfortunately came too late".[^cite_note-283GD-448] The European Union rejected the idea of suspending the [Schengen free travel zone](179592.md).[^cite_note-dw52497811-449][^cite_note-20200313euractiv-450]

### Repatriation of foreign citizens

Main article: [Evacuations related to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evacuations_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)



Ukraine evacuates Ukrainian citizens from [Wuhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan), China.

Several countries repatriated their citizens and diplomatic staff from Wuhan and surrounding areas, primarily through [charter flights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Air_charter). Canada, the United States, Japan, India, Sri Lanka, Australia, France, Argentina, Germany, and Thailand were among the first to do so.[^cite_note-AutoDW-169-451] Brazil and New Zealand evacuated their own nationals and others.[^cite_note-AutoDW-171-452][^cite_note-6l9jG-453] On 14 March, South Africa repatriated 112 South Africans who tested negative, while four who showed symptoms were left behind.[^cite_note-auto3-454] Pakistan declined to evacuate its citizens.[^cite_note-20200131dialoguepakistan-455]

On 15 February, the US announced it would evacuate Americans aboard the *[Diamond Princess](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diamond_Princess_outbreak)* cruise ship,[^cite_note-AutoDW-174-456] and, on 21 February, Canada evacuated 129 Canadians from the ship.[^cite_note-AutoDW-175-457] In early March, the Indian government began repatriating its citizens from Iran.[^cite_note-czjOG-458][^cite_note-indiatimes74647353-459] On 20 March, the United States began to withdraw some troops from Iraq.[^cite_note-USWithdraw-460]

## Impact

Main article: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

Further information: [Social impact of the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

### Economics

Main article: [Economic impact of the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

See also: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on aviation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_aviation), [on science and technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_science_and_technology), [on financial markets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Financial_market_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic), [2020 stock market crash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_stock_market_crash), and [COVID-19 recession](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_recession)



A [stock index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stock_index) chart shows the [2020 stock market crash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_stock_market_crash).

The pandemic and responses to it damaged the global economy. On 27 February 2020, worries about the outbreak crushed US stock indexes, which posted their sharpest falls since 2008.[^cite_note-U7teI-461]



Viewed in context, the price of oil has been affected internationally at the times of the COVID-19 pandemic, the [2022– Russo-Ukrainian war](70149799.md) and the [2026 Iran war](82533582.md).

[^cite_note-EIA_OilPriceData-462]



Industries such as [cruising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cruise_line) experienced a significant decline—down to levels not seen in thirty years.

[^cite_note-Cruise_202412-463]

Tourism collapsed due to travel restrictions, closing of public places including travel attractions, and advice of governments against travel. Airlines cancelled flights, while British regional airline [Flybe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flybe_\(1979%E2%80%932020\)) collapsed.[^cite_note-z2VrM-464] The cruise line industry was hard hit,[^cite_note-smh20200227-465] and train stations and ferry ports closed.[^cite_note-wnn7L-466] International mail stopped or was delayed.[^cite_note-wsj1158868721-467]

The retail sector faced reductions in store hours or closures.[^cite_note-468] Retailers in Europe and Latin America faced traffic declines of 40 per cent. North America and Middle East retailers saw a 50–60 per cent drop.[^cite_note-20200402aislelabs-469] Shopping centres faced a 33–43 per cent drop in foot traffic in March compared to February. Mall operators around the world coped by increasing sanitation, installing thermal scanners to check the temperature of shoppers, and cancelling events.[^cite_note-J6Y6Z-470]

Hundreds of millions of jobs were lost,[^cite_note-aljazeera2004271718-471][^cite_note-20200506csmonitor-472] including more than 40 million jobs in the US.[^cite_note-20200528WaPo-473] According to a report by [Yelp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yelp), about 60% of US businesses that closed will stay shut permanently.[^cite_note-vLHxV-474] The [International Labour Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Labour_Organization) (ILO) reported that the income generated in the first nine months of 2020 from work across the world dropped by 10.7%, or \$3.5 trillion.[^cite_note-rd8bm-475]

#### Supply shortages

Main article: [Shortages related to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)



COVID-19 fears led to panic buying of essentials across the world, including toilet paper, instant noodles, bread, rice, vegetables, disinfectant, and rubbing alcohol (picture taken in February 2020).

Pandemic fears led to [panic buying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Panic_buying), emptying groceries of essentials such as food, toilet paper, and bottled water. Panic buying stemmed from perceived threat, perceived scarcity, fear of the unknown, coping behaviour, and social psychological factors (e.g. [social influence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_influence) and trust).[^cite_note-cSvA4-476]

Supply [shortages](2364729.md) were due to disruption to factory and logistic operations; shortages were worsened by [supply chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supply_chain) disruptions from factory and port shutdowns, and labour shortages.[^cite_note-XvcU9-477]

Shortages continued as managers underestimated the speed of economic recovery after the initial economic crash. The technology industry, in particular, warned of delays from underestimates of [semiconductor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Semiconductor) demand for vehicles and other products.[^cite_note-AutoDW-263-478]

According to WHO Secretary-General Tedros Ghebreyesus, demand for [personal protective equipment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Personal_protective_equipment) (PPE) rose one hundredfold, pushing prices up twentyfold.[^cite_note-AutoDW-275-479][^cite_note-AutoDW-276-480] PPE stocks were exhausted everywhere.[^cite_note-Mycfo-481][^cite_note-482][^cite_note-483]

In September 2021, the [World Bank](36752.md) reported that food prices remained generally stable and the supply outlook remained positive. However, the poorest countries witnessed a sharp increase in food prices, reaching the highest level since the pandemic began.[^cite_note-484][^cite_note-auto4-485] The Agricultural Commodity Price Index stabilised in the third quarter but remained 17% higher than in January 2021.[^cite_note-486][^cite_note-auto4-485]

By contrast, petroleum products were in surplus at the beginning of the pandemic, as demand for gasoline and other products collapsed due to reduced commuting and other trips.[^cite_note-BBC,_US_oil_prices,_21_April_2020-487] The [2021 global energy crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_global_energy_crisis) was driven by a global surge in demand as the world economy recovered. Energy demand was particularly strong in Asia.[^cite_note-488][^cite_note-489]

In May 2026, the US charged seven Chinese executives and four major container manufacturers, Singamas Container Holdings, [China International Marine Containers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_International_Marine_Containers), Shanghai Universal Logistics Equipment, and CXIC Group Containers, with conspiring to restrict shipping container production and fix prices from 2019 to 2024, contributing to global supply chain disruptions and higher consumer costs during the pandemic.[^cite_note-490]

### Arts and cultural heritage

Main article: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on the arts and cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_the_arts_and_cultural_heritage)

The performing arts and [cultural heritage sectors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_heritage_sector) were profoundly affected by the pandemic. Both organisations' and individuals' operations have been impacted globally. By March 2020, around the world and, to varying degrees, museums, libraries, performance venues, and other cultural institutions had been indefinitely closed with their exhibitions, events, and performances cancelled or postponed.[^cite_note-v5Qlx-491] A 2021 [UNESCO](21786641.md) report estimated ten million job losses worldwide in the culture and creative industries.[^cite_note-Ottone-492][^cite_note-493] Some services continued through digital platforms,[^cite_note-ZljIY-494][^cite_note-Burke2020-495][^cite_note-SxIEK-496] such as live streaming concerts[^cite_note-SZ5Mc-497] or web-based arts festivals.[^cite_note-mYlLn-498]

### Politics

Main article: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_politics)

See also: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on international relations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_international_relations)

The pandemic affected political systems, causing suspensions of legislative activities,[^cite_note-20200313nationalpost-499] isolations or deaths of politicians,[^cite_note-20200318foreignpolicy-500] and rescheduled elections.[^cite_note-20200427nytimes-501] Although they developed broad support among epidemiologists, NPIs (non-pharmaceutical interventions) were controversial in many countries. Intellectual opposition came primarily from other fields, along with heterodox epidemiologists.[^cite_note-74OHE-502]

#### Brazil

The pandemic (and the [response of Brazilian politicians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Brazil#Responses) to it) led to widespread panic, confusion, and pessimism in Brazil.[^cite_note-BenderdaSilvaBaum2022-503] When questioned regarding record deaths in the country in April 2020, Brazilian president [Jair Bolsonaro](46603496.md) said "So what? I'm sorry. What do you want me to do about it?"[^cite_note-Conde_2020-504] Bolsonaro disregarded WHO-recommended mitigation techniques and instead [downplayed the risks of the virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Brazil#Comments_by_Bolsonaro), promoted increased economic activity, spread [misinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Brazil#Presidential_responses) about the efficacy of masks, vaccines, and public health measures, and distributed unproven treatments including [hydroxychloroquine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chloroquine_and_hydroxychloroquine_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic) and [ivermectin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ivermectin_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic).[^cite_note-BenderdaSilvaBaum2022-503] A series of [federal health ministers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Ministers_of_Health_of_Brazil) resigned or were dismissed after they refused to implement Bolsonaro's policies.[^cite_note-Londono2020-505]

Disagreements between federal and state governments led to a chaotic and delayed response to the rapid spread of the virus,[^cite_note-Dantas2020-506] exacerbated by preexisting social and economic disparities in the country.[^cite_note-BenderdaSilvaBaum2022-503][^cite_note-CarnutMendesGuerra2020-507] Employment, investment, and valuation of the [Brazilian real](34201323.md) plummeted to record lows.[^cite_note-BenderdaSilvaBaum2022-503][^cite_note-ReutersBrazil2020-508] Brazil was also heavily affected by the Delta and Omicron variants.[^cite_note-AlcantaraNogueiraShuabTosta2022-509] At the height of the outbreak in the spring of 2021, 3,000+ Brazilians were dying per day.[^cite_note-BBCBrazilMarch2021-510][^cite_note-NPRBrazilApril2021-511] Bolsonaro's loss to [Lula da Silva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lula_da_Silva) in the [2022 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Brazilian_general_election) is widely credited to the former's [mishandling of the pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_Jair_Bolsonaro#Response_to_COVID-19_pandemic).[^cite_note-ChomskyPolychroniou2023-512][^cite_note-EconomistBolsonaroLoss2022-513][^cite_note-Milhorance2022October-514]

#### China

Further information: [China–United States relations § COVID-19](277880.md#COVID-19)

Multiple provincial-level administrators of the [Chinese Communist Party](7175.md) (CCP) were dismissed over their handling of quarantine measures. Some commentators claimed this move was intended to protect CCP General Secretary [Xi Jinping](2017814.md).[^cite_note-vLpPa-515] The [US intelligence community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_intelligence_community) claimed that China intentionally under-reported its COVID-19 caseload.[^cite_note-20200402nytimes-516] The Chinese government maintained that it acted swiftly and transparently.[^cite_note-hMc3w-517][^cite_note-518] Journalists and activists in China who reported on the pandemic were detained by authorities,[^cite_note-519][^cite_note-520] including [Zhang Zhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zhang_Zhan), who was arrested and tortured.[^cite_note-521][^cite_note-522]

#### Italy



Palazzo Margherita lit in the Italian flag colours to show solidarity during the COVID-19 pandemic on 26 March 2020

In early March 2020, the Italian government criticised the EU's lack of solidarity with Italy.[^cite_note-523][^cite_note-524][^cite_note-politico-corona-525] On 22 March 2020, after a phone call with Italian Prime Minister [Giuseppe Conte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Giuseppe_Conte), Russian president [Vladimir Putin](32817.md) ordered the [Russian army](25709.md) to send military medics, disinfection vehicles, and other medical equipment to Italy.[^cite_note-reuters219081-526][^cite_note-20200328insideover-527] In early April, Norway and EU states like Romania and Austria started to offer help by sending medical personnel and disinfectant,[^cite_note-eumedicalteams-528] and [European Commission](9974.md) president [Ursula von der Leyen](2991932.md) offered an official apology to the country.[^cite_note-euapology-529]

#### United States



Anti-lockdown [protesters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protests_over_responses_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic) rallied at the [Ohio Statehouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ohio_Statehouse) 20 April 2020.

[^cite_note-Columbus_Dispatch-530]

Beginning in mid-April 2020, protestors objected to government-imposed business closures and restrictions on personal movement and assembly.[^cite_note-5YqAF-531] Simultaneously, [essential workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Essential_workers) protested unsafe conditions and low wages by participating in a brief [general strike](41138909.md).[^cite_note-c114M-532] Some political analysts claimed that the pandemic contributed to US President [Donald Trump](4848272.md)'s [2020](48410011.md) defeat to former Vice President [Joe Biden](145422.md) and Senator [Kamala Harris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamala_Harris).[^cite_note-TrumpChances-533][^cite_note-AtlanticChances-534]

The [COVID-19 pandemic in the United States](63136490.md) prompted calls for the United States to adopt social policies common in other wealthy countries, including [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care), [universal child care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_child_care), [paid sick leave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paid_sick_leave), and higher levels of funding for public health.[^cite_note-NYT_Calls-535][^cite_note-Hill5-536][^cite_note-Ddn2P-537] The [Kaiser Family Foundation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaiser_Family_Foundation) estimated that preventable hospitalisations of unvaccinated Americans in the second half of 2021 cost US\$13.8 billion.[^cite_note-Amin-538]

There were also protest in regard to vaccine mandates in the United States.[^cite_note-539] In January 2022, the US [Supreme Court](31737.md) struck down an [Occupational Safety and Health Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupational_Safety_and_Health_Administration) (OSHA) rule that mandated vaccination or a testing regimen for all companies with greater than 100 employees.[^cite_note-540][^cite_note-541]

#### Other countries

The number of journalists imprisoned or detained increased worldwide; some detentions were related to the pandemic.[^cite_note-542][^cite_note-543] The planned [NATO](21133.md) "[Defender 2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_NATO_exercises#2020)" military exercise in Germany, [Poland](22936.md), and the [Baltic states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baltic_states), the largest NATO war exercise since the end of the [Cold War](325329.md), was held on a reduced scale.[^cite_note-20200320spectator-544][^cite_note-20200319reuters-545]

The Iranian government was heavily affected by the virus, which infected some two dozen parliament members and political figures.[^cite_note-D2Xpc-308][^cite_note-546] Iranian president [Hassan Rouhani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hassan_Rouhani) wrote a public letter to world leaders asking for help on 14 March 2020, due to a lack of access to international markets.[^cite_note-u2rRX-547] Saudi Arabia, which had launched a [military intervention in Yemen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian-led_intervention_in_Yemen) in March 2015, declared a ceasefire.[^cite_note-20200409foreignpolicy-548]

Diplomatic relations between [Japan and South Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japan%E2%80%93South_Korea_relations) worsened.[^cite_note-549] South Korea criticised Japan's "ambiguous and passive quarantine efforts" after Japan announced travellers from South Korea must quarantine for two weeks.[^cite_note-HnqN4-550] South Korean society was initially polarised on president [Moon Jae-in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Jae-in)'s response to the crisis; many Koreans signed petitions calling for Moon's [impeachment](15328.md) or praising his response.[^cite_note-imx38-317]

Some countries passed emergency legislation. Some commentators expressed concern that it could allow governments to strengthen their grip on power.[^cite_note-20200331theguardian-551][^cite_note-20200330nytimes-552] In Hungary, the parliament voted to allow Prime Minister [Viktor Orbán](750582.md) to rule by decree indefinitely, suspend parliament and elections, and punish those deemed to have spread false information.[^cite_note-20200330cnn-553] In countries such as [Egypt](8087628.md),[^cite_note-dw53009293-554] [Turkey](11125639.md),[^cite_note-20200401foreignpolicy-555] and [Thailand](30128.md),[^cite_note-556] opposition activists and government critics were [arrested](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misinformation_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic#Efforts_to_combat_misinformation) for allegedly spreading [fake news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news).[^cite_note-20200410straitstimes-557] In [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_India), journalists criticising the government's response were arrested or issued warnings by police and authorities.[^cite_note-558]

### Food systems

Further information: [Food security during the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_security_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

The pandemic disrupted food systems worldwide,[^cite_note-\:9b-559][^cite_note-560] hitting at a time when hunger and undernourishment were rising- an estimated 690 million people lacked food security in 2019.[^cite_note-\:10-561] Food access fell – driven by falling incomes, lost remittances, and disruptions to food production.[^cite_note-562] In some cases, food prices rose.[^cite_note-\:9b-559][^cite_note-\:10-561] The pandemic and its accompanying lockdowns and travel restrictions slowed movement of food aid. According to the WHO, 811 million people were undernourished in 2020, "likely related to the fallout of COVID-19".[^cite_note-563][^cite_note-Effect_of_COVID-19_on_food_security-440]

### Education

Main article: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_education)



Students take end-of-year exams in [Tabriz](439791.md), Iran, during the pandemic.

The pandemic impacted educational systems in many countries. Many governments temporarily closed educational institutions, often replaced by [online education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online_education). Other countries, such as Sweden, kept their schools open. As of September 2020, approximately 1.077 billion [learners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Learning) were affected due to school closures. School closures impacted students, teachers, and families with far-reaching economic and societal consequences.[^cite_note-564] They shed light on social and economic issues, including [student debt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Student_debt), [digital learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_learning), food insecurity, and [homelessness](19394651.md), as well as access to [childcare](389615.md), health care, housing, internet, and [disability services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disability_rights_movement). The impact was more severe for disadvantaged children.[^cite_note-565] Many countries, including Bangladesh, granted auto promotion to the public examination candidates.[^cite_note-566] The Higher Education Policy Institute reported that around 63% of university students in the UK claimed worsened mental health as a result of the pandemic.[^cite_note-567]

### Health

Main articles: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on other health issues](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_other_health_issues) and [Mental health during the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_health_during_the_COVID-19_pandemic)

The pandemic impacted global health for many conditions. Hospital visits fell.[^cite_note-568] Visits for heart attack symptoms declined by 38% in the US and 40% in Spain.[^cite_note-569] The head of cardiology at the [University of Arizona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Arizona) said, "My worry is some of these people are dying at home because they're too scared to go to the hospital".[^cite_note-Stat_News,_with_serious_heart_symptoms_away,_April_23-570] People with strokes and [appendicitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Appendicitis) were less likely to seek treatment.[^cite_note-571][^cite_note-572][^cite_note-Stat_News,_with_serious_heart_symptoms_away,_April_23-570] [Medical supply shortages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shortages_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic) impacted many people.[^cite_note-Atlantic_Shortages-573] The pandemic impacted [mental health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mental_health),[^cite_note-574][^cite_note-Stix_2021-575] increasing [anxiety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anxiety), depression, and [post-traumatic stress disorder](82974.md), affecting healthcare workers, patients, and quarantined individuals.[^cite_note-576][^cite_note-Santomauro_2021-577]

In late 2022, during the first northern hemisphere autumn and winter seasons following the widespread relaxation of global public health measures, North America and Europe experienced a surge in respiratory viruses and coinfections in both adults and children.[^cite_note-578] This formed the beginning of the 2022–2023 paediatric care crisis and what some experts termed a "[tripledemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tripledemic)" of seasonal influenza, [respiratory syncytial virus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Respiratory_syncytial_virus) (RSV), and SARS-CoV-2 throughout North America.[^cite_note-579][^cite_note-580] In the United Kingdom, paediatric infections also began to spike beyond pre-pandemic levels, albeit with different illnesses, such as [Group A streptococcal infection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Group_A_streptococcal_infection) and resultant [scarlet fever](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scarlet_fever).[^cite_note-581][^cite_note-United_Kingdom-582] As of mid-December 2022, 19 children in the UK had died due to [Strep A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streptococcus_pyogenes) and the wave of infections had begun to spread into North America and Mainland Europe.[^cite_note-Strep_A_death-583][^cite_note-Strep_Expansion-584]

The B/Yamagata lineage of [influenza B](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Influenza_B) might have become extinct in 2020/2021 due to COVID-19 pandemic measures.[^cite_note-Alhoufie2021-585][^cite_note-Koutsakos2021-586] There have been no naturally occurring cases confirmed since March 2020.[^cite_note-WHOflu2023-587][^cite_note-UMN2023-588] In 2023, the WHO concluded that protection against the Yamagata lineage was no longer necessary in the seasonal [flu vaccine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flu_vaccine), reducing the number of lineages targeted by the vaccine from four to three.[^cite_note-WHOflu2023-587][^cite_note-UMN2023-588]

Preventative measures taken during the COVID-19 pandemic may have impacted the number of [bronchiolitis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronchiolitis) cases, with observed decreases in cases during the pandemic followed by an increase when preventative measures were rolled back.[^cite_note-Sabeena-589][^cite_note-Hogan2025-590]

### Environment

Main article: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on the environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_the_environment)



Images from the [NASA Earth Observatory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NASA_Earth_Observatory) show a stark drop in pollution in [Wuhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuhan), when comparing [NO<sub>2</sub>](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitrogen_dioxide) levels in early 2019 (top) and early 2020 (bottom).

[^cite_note-591]

The pandemic and the reaction to it positively affected the [environment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_environment) and [climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate) as a result of reduced human activity. During the "[anthropause](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthropause)", fossil fuel use decreased, resource consumption declined, and waste disposal improved, generating less pollution.[^cite_note-\:9-592] [Planned air travel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic#Transportation) and vehicle transportation declined. In China, [lockdowns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_lockdown_in_Hubei) and other measures resulted in a 26% decrease in coal consumption, and a 50% reduction in nitrogen oxides emissions.[^cite_note-\:9-592][^cite_note-593][^cite_note-594]

In 2020, a worldwide study on mammalian wildlife responses to human presence during COVID lockdowns found complex patterns of animal behaviour. Carnivores were generally less active when humans were around, while herbivores in developed areas were more active. Among other findings, this suggested that herbivores may view humans as a shield against predators, highlighting the importance of location and human presence history in understanding wildlife responses to changes in human activity in a given area.[^cite_note-595]

A [wide variety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_animals_that_can_get_SARS-CoV-2) of largely mammalian species, both captive and wild, have been shown to be susceptible to SARS-CoV-2, with some encountering a particularly high degree of fatal outcomes.[^cite_note-596] In particular, both [farmed and wild mink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_in_mink) have developed highly symptomatic and severe COVID-19 infections, with a mortality rate as high as 35–55% according to one study.[^cite_note-Frontiers_Spread-597][^cite_note-598] [White-tailed deer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SARS-CoV-2_in_white-tailed_deer), on the other hand, have largely avoided severe outcomes but have effectively become [natural reservoirs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_reservoir) of the virus, with large numbers of free-ranging deer infected throughout the US and Canada, including approximately 80% of [Iowa](26810748.md)'s wild deer herd.[^cite_note-599][^cite_note-Nature_April-600] An August 2023 study appeared to confirm the status of white-tailed deer as a disease reservoir, noting that the viral evolution of SARS-CoV-2 in deer occurs at triple the rate of its evolution in humans and that infection rates remained high, even in areas rarely frequented by humans.[^cite_note-Triple_Evolution-601]

### Discrimination and prejudice

Main article: [Xenophobia and racism related to the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xenophobia_and_racism_related_to_the_COVID-19_pandemic)



A socially distanced homeless encampment in San Francisco, California, in May 2020

[^cite_note-602]

Heightened prejudice, [xenophobia](34141.md), and racism toward people of [Chinese and East Asian descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinophobe) were documented around the world.[^cite_note-AutoDW-251-603][^cite_note-zogcf-604] Reports from February 2020, when most confirmed cases were confined to China, cited racist sentiments about Chinese people "deserving" the virus.[^cite_note-bangkokpost1854094-605][^cite_note-psychologytoday202002-606][^cite_note-20200201telegraph-607] Individuals of Asian descent in Europe and North America reported increasing instances of racially motivated abuse and assaults as a result of the pandemic.[^cite_note-NYT_Racism-608][^cite_note-05O7n-609][^cite_note-NYT_April_Racism-610] US president [Donald Trump](4848272.md) was criticised for referring to SARS-CoV-2 as the "Chinese Virus" and "Kung Flu", terms which were condemned as being racist and xenophobic.[^cite_note-trumpnyt-611][^cite_note-20200320BI-612][^cite_note-MNCw4-613]

[Age-based discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ageism) against older adults increased during the pandemic. This was attributed to their perceived vulnerability and subsequent physical and social isolation measures, which, coupled with their reduced social activity, increased dependency on others. Similarly, limited digital literacy left the elderly more vulnerable to isolation, depression, and loneliness.[^cite_note-614]

In a correspondence published in *[The Lancet](463899.md)* in 2021, German epidemiologist Günter Kampf described the harmful effects of "inappropriate stigmatisation of unvaccinated people, who include our patients, colleagues, and other fellow citizens", noting the evidence that vaccinated individuals play a large role in transmission.[^cite_note-615] American bioethicist Arthur Caplan responded to Kampf, writing "Criticising \[the unvaccinated] who... wind up in hospitals and morgues in huge numbers, put stress on finite resources, and prolong the pandemic... is not stigmatising, it is deserved moral condemnation".[^cite_note-616]

In January 2022, [Amnesty International](18947898.md) urged [Italy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Italy) to change their anti-COVID-19 restrictions to avoid discrimination against unvaccinated people, saying that "the government must continue to ensure that the entire population can enjoy its fundamental rights". The restrictions included mandatory vaccination over the age of 50, and mandatory vaccination to use public transport.[^cite_note-617]

### Lifestyle changes



The "Wee Annie" statue in [Gourock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gourock,_Scotland), Scotland, was given a face mask during the pandemic.

The pandemic triggered massive changes in behaviour, from increased Internet commerce to cultural changes in the workplace. Online retailers in the US posted \$791.70 billion in sales in 2020, an increase of 32.4% from \$598.02 billion the year before.[^cite_note-DDCV1ECMR-618] Home delivery orders increased, while indoor restaurant dining shut down due to lockdown orders or low sales.[^cite_note-Fo18zrty783-619][^cite_note-Fo18zrty784-620] Hackers, [cybercriminals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cybercriminal), and scammers took advantage of the changes to launch new online attacks.[^cite_note-Fo18zrty787-621]

Education in some countries temporarily shifted from physical attendance to video conferencing.[^cite_note-Fo18zrty790-622] Massive layoffs shrank the airline, travel, hospitality, and other industries.[^cite_note-Fo18zrty6966-623][^cite_note-Fo18zrty9999-624] Despite most corporations implementing measures to address COVID-19 in the workplace, a poll from [Catalyst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catalyst_\(nonprofit_organization\)) found that as many as 68% of employees around the world felt that these policies were only performative and "not genuine".[^cite_note-625]

The pandemic led to a surge in [remote work](158555.md). According to a [Gallup poll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gallup_poll), only 4% of US employees were fully remote before the pandemic, compared to 43% in May 2020. Among white collar workers, that shift was more pronounced, with 6% increasing to 65% in the same period.[^cite_note-The_New_York_Times_2022-626] That trend continued in later stages of the pandemic, with many workers choosing to remain remote even after workplaces reopened.[^cite_note-Mitchell_2022-627][^cite_note-Kagubare_2023-628] Many Nordic, European, and Asian companies increased their recruitment of international remote workers even as the pandemic waned, partially to save on labour costs.[^cite_note-ComputerWeekly.com_2023-629][^cite_note-Braesemann_Stephany_Teutloff_Kässi_2022_p=e0274630-630] This also led to a talent drain in the global south and in remote areas in the global north.[^cite_note-Braesemann_Stephany_Teutloff_Kässi_2022_p=e0274630-630][^cite_note-EuropeanStingWEF2023-631] High cost of living and dense urban areas also lost office real estate value due to remote worker exodus.[^cite_note-Economist2022Jan-632] By May 2023, due to increasing layoffs and concerns over productivity, some white collar workplaces in the US had resorted to performance review penalties and indirect incentives (e.g. donations to charity) to encourage workers to return to the office.[^cite_note-Peck_2023-633]

### Historiography

A 2021 study noted that the COVID-19 pandemic had increased interest in epidemics and infectious diseases among both historians and the general public. Prior to the pandemic, these topics were usually overlooked by "general" history and only received attention in the [history of medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_medicine).[^cite_note-634] Many comparisons were made between the COVID-19 and [1918 influenza pandemics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1918_influenza_pandemic),[^cite_note-635][^cite_note-636] including the development of anti-mask movements,[^cite_note-637][^cite_note-638] the widespread promotion of misinformation[^cite_note-639][^cite_note-640] and the impact of [socioeconomic disparities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_determinants_of_health).[^cite_note-641]

### Religion

Main article: [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_religion)

Two men in masks, wearing mitres and red vestments, stand in front of an altar. Altar servers, deacons, and priests in the background similarly wear masks.

[Jorge Ortiga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jorge_Ortiga), [Archbishop of Braga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Archdiocese_of_Braga), Portugal, wearing a protective mask during [Pentecost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentecost) Mass in May 2020

In some areas, religious groups exacerbated the spread of the virus, through large gatherings and the dissemination of misinformation.[^cite_note-642][^cite_note-643][^cite_note-644] Some religious leaders decried what they saw as violations of religious freedom.[^cite_note-645] In other cases, religious identity was a beneficial factor for health, increasing compliance with public health measures and protecting against the negative effects of isolation on mental wellbeing.[^cite_note-646][^cite_note-647][^cite_note-648]

## Information dissemination

Further information: [Media coverage of the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_coverage_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic), [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on social media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_social_media), and [Impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on journalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_journalism)

Some news organisations removed their online paywalls for some or all of their pandemic-related articles and posts.[^cite_note-649] Many scientific publishers provided pandemic-related journal articles to the public free of charge as part of the National Institutes of Health's COVID-19 Public Health Emergency Initiative.[^cite_note-650][^cite_note-651] According to one estimate from researchers at the University of Rome, 89.5% of COVID-19-related papers were open access, compared to an average of 48.8% for the ten most deadly human diseases.[^cite_note-652] The share of papers published on preprint servers prior to peer review increased dramatically.[^cite_note-653]

During the pandemic, [Web GIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Web_GIS) technology was leveraged to provide up to date visualisations of data related to the pandemic with the public.[^cite_note-Juergens2020-654] Employing this technology, the [Johns Hopkins University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johns_Hopkins_University) COVID-19 [dashboard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dashboard_\(computing\)) served as the first global visualisation of COVID-19 data, which established it as the default method for government agencies to dissemeniate relevant spatial information.[^cite_note-Everts2020-655][^cite_note-Adams2025-656] These dashboards were described by Jonathan Everts as "the most striking cultural artefact of the current coronavirus (SARS-CoV-2) pandemic",[^cite_note-Everts2020-655] and during the pandemic every state government in the United States maintained one.[^cite_note-Adams2023-657]

### Influencer marketing

Many governments made use of [influencer marketing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Influencer_marketing) campaigns in an effort to promote vaccination and safety guidelines such as masks, social distancing and testing. Government funded influencers included celebrities and social media influencers on TikTok, Instagram and Snapchat.[^cite_note-658][^cite_note-659][^cite_note-660]

### Misinformation

Main article: [COVID-19 misinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_misinformation)

[Misinformation](2203174.md) and [conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theory) about the pandemic have been widespread; they travel through [mass media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_coverage_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic), [social media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impact_of_the_COVID-19_pandemic_on_social_media#Misinformation) and text messaging.[^cite_note-FTTextMsg-661] In March 2020, WHO declared an "[infodemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infodemic)" of incorrect information.[^cite_note-Lowy-662] [Cognitive biases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognitive_bias), such as [confirmation bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_bias), are linked to conspiracy beliefs, including [COVID-19 vaccine hesitancy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_vaccine_hesitancy).[^cite_note-663]

## Culture and society

Further information: [COVID-19 pandemic in popular culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_popular_culture)

The COVID-19 pandemic had a major impact on popular culture. It was included in the narratives of ongoing pre-pandemic television series and become a central narrative in new ones, with mixed results.[^cite_note-664] Writing for *[The New York Times](30680.md)* about the then-upcoming [BBC](19344654.md) sitcom *[Pandemonium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Here_We_Go_\(TV_series\)#Pilot)* on 16 December 2020, [David Segal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Segal_\(journalist\)) asked, "Are we ready to laugh about Covid-19? Or rather, is there anything amusing, or recognizable in a humorous way, about life during a plague, with all of its indignities and setbacks, not to mention its rituals and rules".[^cite_note-665]

The pandemic had driven some people to seek peaceful [escapism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Escapism) in media, while others were drawn toward fictional pandemics (e.g. [zombie apocalypses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zombie_apocalypse)) as an alternate form of escapism.[^cite_note-666] Common themes have included [contagion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contagious_disease), [isolation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isolation_\(health_care\)) and loss of [control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Control_\(psychology\)).[^cite_note-667] Many drew comparisons to the fictional film *[Contagion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Contagion_\(2011_film\))* (2011),[^cite_note-668][^cite_note-669] praising its accuracies while noting some differences,[^cite_note-670] such as the lack of an orderly vaccine rollout.[^cite_note-671][^cite_note-672]

As people turned to music to relieve emotions evoked by the pandemic, [Spotify](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spotify) listenership showed that classical, [ambient](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambient_music), and [children's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_music) genres grew, while pop, [country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_music), and dance remained relatively stable.[^cite_note-673]

## Transition to later phases

Main article: [Endemic COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic_COVID-19)

A March 2022 review declared a transition to endemic status to be "inevitable".[^cite_note-674] In June 2022, an article in *[Human Genomics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Genome_Organisation)* said that the pandemic was still "raging", but that "now is the time to explore the transition from the pandemic to the endemic phase".[^cite_note-675] Another review that month predicted that the virus that causes COVID-19 would become the fifth endemic seasonal coronavirus, alongside four other [human coronaviruses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/HCoV).[^cite_note-676] A February 2023 review of the four [common cold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_cold) coronaviruses concluded that the virus would become seasonal and, like the common cold, cause less severe disease for most people.[^cite_note-677] Another 2023 review stated that the transition to endemic COVID-19 may take years or decades.[^cite_note-678]

On 5 May 2023, the WHO declared that the pandemic was no longer a [public health emergency of international concern](42694003.md).[^cite_note-679] This led several media outlets to incorrectly report that this meant the pandemic was "over". The WHO commented to [Full Fact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Full_Fact) that it was unlikely to declare the pandemic over "in the near future" and mentioned [cholera](7591.md), which it considers to have been a [pandemic since 1961](16948770.md) (i.e., continuously for the last 65 years).[^cite_note-ff-680] The WHO does not have an official category for pandemics or make declarations of when pandemics start or end.[^cite_note-reuters-4][^cite_note-guardian-260][^cite_note-681][^cite_note-timemarch2024-15] In June 2023, [Hans Kluge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans_Kluge), director of the WHO in Europe, commented that "While the international public health emergency may have ended, the pandemic certainly has not".[^cite_note-kluge-682]

Epidemics and pandemics usually end when the disease becomes [endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic_COVID-19), and when the disease becomes "an accepted, manageable part of normal life in a given society".[^cite_note-charters2021-16] As of March 2024, there was no widely agreed definition of when a disease is or is not a pandemic, though efforts at a formal definition were underway. Experts asked by *[Time](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Time_\(magazine\))* that month noted that COVID-19 continued to circulate and cause disease, but expressed uncertainty as to whether it should still be described as a pandemic.[^cite_note-timemarch2024-15] In December 2024, [Director-General of the World Health Organization](63815422.md) [Tedros Adhanom Ghebreyesus](39712205.md) stated that COVID should not be referred to in past tense as it is "still with us, still causes acute disease and [Long COVID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Long_COVID) and still kills... The world might want to forget about COVID-19, but we cannot afford to."[^cite_note-pasttense-who-683]

By 2025, five years after the start of the pandemic, experts asked by *[The Independent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Independent)* and *[The Washington Post](102226.md)* generally considered COVID-19 to have become endemic.[^cite_note-\:0-13][^cite_note-\:3-14] Cases and deaths from COVID-19 still remained high; in the United States, the only two infectious diseases causing more than 10,000 deaths a year were flu and COVID, according to an expert in *The Washington Post*.[^cite_note-\:0-13]

## Long-term effects

### Economic

Despite strong economic rebounds following the initial lockdowns in early 2020, toward the latter phases of the pandemic, many countries began to experience long-term economic effects. Several countries saw high [inflation rates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inflation_rate) which had global impacts, particularly in developing countries.[^cite_note-684] Some economic impacts such as [supply chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supply_chain) and trade operations were seen as more permanent as the pandemic exposed major weaknesses in these systems.[^cite_note-685]

In Australia, the pandemic caused an increase in [occupational burnout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupational_burnout) in 2022.[^cite_note-686]

During the pandemic, a large percentage of workers in Canada came to prefer working from home, which had an impact on the traditional work model. Some corporations made efforts to force workers to return to work on-site, while some embraced the idea.[^cite_note-687]

### Travel

There was a "travel boom" causing air travel to recover at rates faster than anticipated, and the aviation industry became profitable in 2023 for the first time since 2019, before the pandemic.[^cite_note-688] However, economic issues meant some predicted that the boom would begin to slow down.[^cite_note-689] Business travel on airlines was still below pre-pandemic levels and is predicted not to recover.[^cite_note-690]

### Health

An increase in excess deaths from underlying causes not related to COVID-19 has been largely blamed on systematic issues causing delays in health care and screening during the pandemic, which has resulted in an increase of non-COVID-19 related deaths.[^cite_note-691]

### Immunisations

During the pandemic, millions of children missed out on vaccinations as countries focused efforts on combating COVID-19. Efforts were made to increase vaccination rates among children in [low-income countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Low-income_countries). These efforts were successful in increasing vaccination rates for some diseases, though the UN noted that post-pandemic [measles vaccinations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Measles_vaccination) were still falling behind.[^cite_note-692]

Some of the decrease in immunisation was driven by an increase in mistrust of public health officials. This was seen in both low-income and high-income countries. Several [African countries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_countries) saw a decline in vaccinations due to misinformation around the pandemic flowing into other areas.[^cite_note-693] Immunisation rates have yet to recover in the United States[^cite_note-694] and the United Kingdom.[^cite_note-695]

- [COVID-19 Projections](https://covid19.healthdata.org/global) for many countries published by [Institute for Health Metrics and Evaluation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institute_for_Health_Metrics_and_Evaluation)

- [Coronavirus (COVID-19)](https://www.nejm.org/coronavirus) by *[The New England Journal of Medicine](486492.md)*

- [Coronavirus (COVID-19) Hub](https://www.bmj.com/coronavirus) by [BMJ Publishing Group](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMJ_\(company\))

- [Coronavirus Disease 2019 (COVID-19)](https://jamanetwork.com/journals/jama/pages/coronavirus-alert) by *[JAMA: The Journal of the American Medical Association](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/JAMA_\(journal\))*

- [COVID-19: Novel Coronavirus Outbreak](https://novel-coronavirus.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/); [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20200924195411/https://novel-coronavirus.onlinelibrary.wiley.com/) 24 September 2020 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine) by [Wiley Publishing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wiley_\(publisher\))

- [COVID-19 pandemic (2019–20) Collection](https://collections.plos.org/covid-19) by [Public Library of Science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Library_of_Science) (PLOS)

- [COVID-19 Portfolio](https://icite.od.nih.gov/covid19/search/); [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20200811123243/https://icite.od.nih.gov/covid19/search/) 11 August 2020 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine), a curated collection of publications and preprints by [National Institutes of Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institutes_of_Health) (NIH)

- [COVID-19 Research Highlights](https://www.springernature.com/gp/researchers/campaigns/coronavirus) by [Springer Nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Springer_Nature)

- [COVID-19 Resource Centre](https://www.thelancet.com/coronavirus) by *[The Lancet](463899.md)*

- [Novel Coronavirus Information Center](https://www.elsevier.com/connect/coronavirus-information-center) by [Elsevier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elsevier)

[^cite_note-Barcelona-166]: &#x20;Anna Llupià, Rodríguez-Giralt, Anna Fité, Lola Álamo, Laura de la Torre, Ana Redondo, Mar Callau, and Caterina Guinovart (2020) *[What Is a Zero-COVID Strategy](https://www.isglobal.org/documents/10179/7943094/26_ISGlobal+COVID19+y+COVIDCero+o+Maxima+Supresion+EN/0a4e83bb-6257-4f5d-8960-16c323b464b2)*, [Archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20220103205923/https://www.isglobal.org/documents/10179/7943094/26_ISGlobal+COVID19+y+COVIDCero+o+Maxima+Supresion+EN/0a4e83bb-6257-4f5d-8960-16c323b464b2) 3 January 2022 at the [Wayback Machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayback_Machine), Barcelona Institute for Global Health – COVID-19 & response strategy. "*The strategy of control and maximum suppression (zero-COVID) has been implemented successfully in a number of countries. The objective of this strategy is to keep transmission of the virus as close to zero as possible and ultimately to eliminate it entirely from particular geographical areas. The strategy aims to increase the capacity to identify and trace chains of transmission and to identify and manage outbreaks, while also integrating economic, psychological, social, and healthcare support to guarantee the isolation of cases and contacts. This approach is also known as "Find, Test, Trace, Isolate and Support" (FTTIS)*"

Are we ready to laugh about Covid-19? Or rather, is there anything amusing, or recognizable in a humorous way, about life during a plague, with all of its indignities and setbacks, not to mention its rituals (clapping for [health care workers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_worker)) and rules ([face masks](63631542.md), please).

[Australian Broadcasting Corporation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Broadcasting_Corporation). Retrieved 18 December 2020. Fictitious stories about pandemics give us a way to experience the horror in a controlled way, with the pacing we've grown to expect, where resolution is always possible, and where we can always turn off the TV if it gets a bit too much.

[doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_\(identifier\)):[10.3390/microorganisms11020445](https://doi.org/10.3390%2Fmicroorganisms11020445). [ISSN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISSN_\(identifier\)) [2076-2607](https://search.worldcat.org/issn/2076-2607). [PMC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMC_\(identifier\)) [9961755](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC9961755). [PMID](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PMID_\(identifier\)) [36838410](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/36838410). After evaluating the biology, pathogenesis, and emergence of the human coronaviruses that cause the common cold, we can anticipate that with increased vaccine immunity to SARS-CoV-2, it will become a seasonal, endemic coronavirus that causes less severe disease in most individuals. Much like the common cold CoVs, the potential for severe disease will likely be present in those who lack a protective immune response or are immunocompromised.
